Learning general Gaussian mixtures with efficient score matching
Sitan Chen, Vasilis Kontonis, Kulin Shah

Abstract
We study the problem of learning mixtures of k k Gaussians in d d dimensions. We make no separation assumptions on the underlying mixture components: we only require that the covariance matrices have bounded condition number and that the means and covariances lie in a ball of bounded radius. We give an algorithm that draws d poly ⁡ ( k / " ) d^{\mathrm{poly}(k/\varepsilon)} samples from the target mixture, runs in sample-polynomial time, and constructs a sampler whose output distribution is " \varepsilon -close from the unknown mixture in total variation. Prior works for this problem either (i) required exponential runtime in the dimension d d , (ii) placed strong assumptions on the instance (e.g., spherical covariances or clusterability), or (iii) had doubly exponential dependence on the number of components k k . Our approach departs from commonly used techniques for this problem like the method of moments. Instead, we leverage a recently developed reduction, based on diffusion models, from distribution learning to a supervised learning task called score matching. We give an algorithm for the latter by proving a structural result showing that the score function of a Gaussian mixture can be approximated by a piecewise-polynomial function, and there is an efficient algorithm for finding it. To our knowledge, this is the first example of diffusion models achieving a state-of-the-art theoretical guarantee for an unsupervised learning task.



1
Introduction

Gaussian mixture models (GMMs) are
one of the most well-studied models in statistics,
with a history going back to the work of Pearson
[
Pea94
]
.
Its computational study was initiated in the work of Dasgupta
[
Das99a
]
; since then, it has been one of the prototypical non-convex learning problems that
has attracted significant attention from the theoretical computer science community

[
VW02
,
KSV05
,
BV08a
,
KMV10
,
MV10
,
BS15
,
HL18
,
KSS18
,
DHKK20
,
BK20
,
DK20
,
LL22
,
LM23
,
BDJ+22
,
BS23
]
.

Learning without separation

We focus on learning even when parameter recovery is impossible, i.e., without assuming that the
components of the mixture are separated. In this setting, the learner has to produce a hypothesis that is close to the target GMM in total variation distance
[
FOS08
,
MV10
,
CDSS13
,
SOAJ14
,
DK14
,
DKK+16
,
ADLS17
,
LS17
,
ABDH+18
,
DK20
,
BDJ+22
,
BS23
]
.

Statistically, this problem is essentially completely understood: in order to approximate the target mixture of
k
Gaussians in
\varepsilon
total variation distance, it is known that
\widetilde{\Theta}(kd^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples are sufficient and also necessary
[
ABDH+18
]
. Even though statistically almost optimal, the algorithm of
[
ABDH+18
]
has a runtime scaling exponentially in
\tilde{O}(kd^{2})
. This exponential dependence on the dimension is due to the fact that their algorithm is based on brute-force enumeration.

Despite significant efforts, the computational aspects of the problem are still far from
well-understood. The work
[
SOAJ14
]
provided an algorithm for learning
mixtures of spherical (i.e., with covariance matrices that are multiples of the identity
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
)
with
\mathrm{poly}(dk/\varepsilon)
sample complexity and
\mathrm{poly}(d)(k/\varepsilon)^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}
runtime.
For spherical Gaussians, the runtime was more recently improved to quasi-polynomial in
k
:
in
[
DK20
]
, a runtime and sample complexity of
\mathrm{poly}(d)(k/\varepsilon)^{\log^{2}k}
was given.

For GMMs with general covariance matrices, the focus of the present work, the best-known runtime is due to

[
BDJ+22
]
and is doubly exponential in the number of components
k
, i.e.,

(d/\varepsilon)^{k}{{(1/\varepsilon)}^{k}}^{k^{2}}
. To the best of our knowledge, this doubly
exponential dependency on
k
is implicit in all works on learning general GMMs using the method of moments
[
MV10
,
BK20
,
DHKK20
,
LM23
]
(see
Section

2.5
for intuition for where this comes from).

In particular, for any
k=\Omega(\sqrt{\log d})
, previously there was no algorithm that ran in time faster than the exponential-time algorithm of
[
ABDH+18
]
, even for constant
\epsilon
!

On the negative side, there is strong evidence in the form of statistical query (SQ)
[
DKS17
]
and lattice-based
[
BRST21
,
GVV22
]
hardness that runtime which scales super-polynomially in the number of components
k
is necessary. More precisely, the SQ lower bound of
[
DKS17
]
implies that even to learn within constant
accuracy
\varepsilon>0
,
d^{\Omega(k)}
runtime is required. Our work aims to bridge the gaps
between the best-known upper and lower bounds for learning GMMs – we ask the following fundamental question.

What is the best possible runtime for learning general Gaussian mixture models with
k
components?
Can we improve over the doubly exponential runtime of moment-based methods?

We make significant progress towards answering this question.
Under mild “condition number” bounds on the mixture components – and without assuming the components are separated – we give an algorithm that achieves runtime
d^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}
for any constant accuracy
\varepsilon>0
. Thus, for well-conditioned mixtures, our result
improves
exponentially
over the best-known runtime of
[
BDJ+22
]
in the regime where
k=\Omega(\sqrt{\log d})
.

Diffusion models and learning

Interestingly, our algorithm
does not rely on matching moments
with
the target mixture. Instead, we draw inspiration from the recent literature on proving theoretical guarantees for diffusion models
[
DBTHD21
,
BMR22
,
CLL22
,
DB22
,
LLT22
,
LWYL22
,
Pid22
,
WY22
,
CCL+23b
,
CDD23
,
LLT23
,
LWCC23
,
BDD23
,
CCL+23a
,
BDBDD23
,
CDS23
,
WWY24
]
, the state-of-the-art method in practice for audio and image generation
[
SDWMG15
,
DN21
,
SSDK+20
,
HJA20
]
. These works culminated in the key finding that for any distribution with bounded second moment, there is a reduction from distribution learning to a supervised learning task called
score matching
. Roughly speaking, this task is defined as follows: given a sample from the target distribution that has been corrupted by some Gaussian noise, predict the noise that was used to generate the sample (see
Section

2.1
for an exposition of these concepts). Despite the striking level of generality with which this reduction holds, these works fell short of giving “end-to-end” learning guarantees as they didn’t address how to actually perform score matching algorithmically.

Our main technical contribution is an algorithm for score matching for GMMs. This relies on a novel structural result showing that the score function of a GMM can be well-approximated by a piecewise polynomial, together with an efficient procedure to recover the polynomial pieces.

While diffusion models have achieved remarkable empirical successes
[
BGJ+23
]
, to our knowledge our guarantee marks the first example of an unsupervised learning problem where diffusion models can even yield improved
theoretical
guarantees. Our techniques are a synthesis of this modern algorithmic technique on the one hand and classic ideas from theoretical computer science like low-degree approximation on the other. We leave it as an intriguing open question to identify other problems for which this marriage of toolkits could prove useful.

1.1
Our results and techniques

We first give the formal definition of the well-conditioned GMMs that we consider in this work.
Roughly, we require that the covariance matrices of the components are well-conditioned in the sense that their eigenvalues are upper and lower bounded and that the means and covariances lie within an
\ell_{2}
ball
of bounded radius.

Definition 1.1

(Well-Conditioned Gaussian Mixture)
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
be
d
-dimensional Gaussian distributions
with means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}
and covariances
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k}
. We denote by
\mathcal{M}
the mixture of these distributions with weights
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
. We will say that
\mathcal{M}
is
\tau
-well-conditioned if
for some
\alpha\leq 1\leq\beta
and
R>0
with
(\beta/\alpha)\log R\leq\tau
, it holds that: for all
i
,
\alpha~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{F}\leq R
.
When we want to distinguish between parameters we will also say that
\mathcal{M}
is

(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned.
Moreover, we denote by
\lambda_{\rm min}
the minimum weight
\min_{i\in[k]}\lambda_{i}
.

We now present our main result: an efficient algorithm for learning well-conditioned GMMs.

Theorem 1.2

(Informal – Learning Gaussian mixtures, see
Theorem

3.2
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
\tau
-well-conditioned mixture of
k
Gaussians in
d

dimensions, and suppose
\lambda_{\rm min}\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(k)
.
There exists an algorithm that draws
N=d^{\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/\varepsilon)}
samples from
\mathcal{M}
, runs in sample-polynomial time, and constructs a sampling oracle
whose output distribution is
\varepsilon
-close to
\mathcal{M}
in total variation. To generate a new sample the oracle requires
\mathrm{poly}(N,d)
time.

To our knowledge, this is the first example of an unsupervised learning problem for which a diffusion-based sampler outperforms existing state-of-the-art theoretical approaches
[
MV10
,
BDJ+22
]
. In particular, when the number of components
k
is super-constant, i.e.,
k=\Omega(\sqrt{\log d})
, we obtain a quasipolynomial
2^{\mathrm{poly}(\log d)}
runtime, improving over the exponential
2^{\mathrm{poly}(d)}
runtime following from
[
BDJ+22
]
. Moreover, we remark that using moment methods for Gaussian mixtures, e.g.,
[
BDJ+22
]
, results in a doubly exponential runtime in
k
even for well-conditioned mixtures, see
Section

2.5
.
Finally, our improvements hold for any
\epsilon=1/\mathrm{polylog}(d)
. In fact, prior to our work, nothing better than doubly
exponential in
k
was known even for constant accuracy
\epsilon=\Omega(1)
. We leave investigating whether the dependency on
1/\epsilon
can be improved as an interesting question for future work.

Learning mixtures of degenerate Gaussians.

As stated,
Theorem

1.2
does not appear to give anything for mixtures with covariances that are not full rank. This includes, for instance, mixtures of linear regressions and mixtures of linear subspaces
[
CLS20
,
DK20
]
. It turns out that we can still give a learning guarantees in this case, though in
Wasserstein distance
rather than total variation, see
Remark

2.2
.

1.2
Related work

Learning mixtures of Gaussians

A thorough literature review on learning Gaussian mixtures is well outside the scope of this work. In addition to the sampling of works
[
Das99b
,
FOS08
,
MV10
,
BS15
,
CDSS13
,
SOAJ14
,
DK14
,
DKK+16
,
ADLS17
,
LS17
,
ABDH+18
,
DK20
,
BK20
,
DHKK20
,
BDJ+22
,
BS23
]
mentioned in the introduction which deal with parameter estimation or distribution learning, we also mention a related line of work on
clustering
Gaussian mixtures. This is a setting where there is a large enough separation between components that one can reliably identify which component generated a given sample. Some representative works in this line include
[
VW04
,
BV08b
,
RV17
,
HL18
,
DKS18
,
KSS18
,
LL22
]
.

Similar in spirit to the present work is the interesting work of
[
YWR23
]
which also eschews the method of moments in favor of a variational method. Whereas we use diffusion models, they use a certain interacting particle system that approximates a Wasserstein gradient flow. They focus on the case of Gaussian mixtures with identity covariance components. While they prove that the gradient flow itself converges in an asymptotic sense and numerically demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach, they do not prove non-asymptotic, end-to-end learning guarantees like in the present work.

General theory for diffusion models

Several works have provided convergence guarantees for DDPMs and variants
[
DBTHD21
,
BMR22
,
CLL22
,
DB22
,
LLT22
,
LWYL22
,
Pid22
,
WY22
,
CCL+23b
,
CDD23
,
LLT23
,
LWCC23
,
BDD23
,
CCL+23a
,
BDBDD23
]
. These works assume the existence of an oracle for accurate score estimation and show that diffusion models can learn essentially any distribution over
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
(e.g.
[
CCL+23b
,
LLT23
]
show this for arbitrary compactly supported distributions, and
[
CLL22
,
BDBDD23
]
extended this to arbitrary distributions with finite second moment). Recently,
[
KV23
]
showed that Langevin diffusion with data-dependent initialization can also learn multimodal distributions like mixtures of Gaussians, provided one can perform score matching. In another sampling context,
[
AHL+23
,
ACV24
]
gave fast parallel algorithms based on a similar diffusion-style sampler for various problems like Eulerian tours and asymmetric determinantal point processes.

End-to-end applications of diffusions

In this work, we use a diffusion process as a tool to obtain
end-to-end efficient learning algorithms
and we are not making “black-box” assumptions about the computational or the statistical complexity of learning the score function.
The recent works
[
SCK23
,
CKVEZ23
]
also consider learning Gaussian mixtures, specifically with well-separated identity covariance components, using diffusions and show in different settings that gradient descent can provably perform score matching.
The results of
[
SCK23
,
CKVEZ23
]
only apply to the special
case of learning spherical Gaussian mixtures — a setting that is already known to admit
efficient learning algorithms. The focus of those works is mainly in understanding why gradient descent for score matching can achieve guarantees similar to the prior known results while our goal in this work is to provide new efficient algorithms for general mixtures that are not captured by prior works.

Several recent results use diffusion models to obtain new
sampling
algorithms
with a focus on graphical models. This is a different setting than the one considered in the present work: instead of being given samples from the target distribution, one is given a Hamiltonian describing some graphical model, or some combinatorial object such that one would like to sample certain structures defined on it.
For example,
[
EAMS22
,
MW23b
,
AMS23
,
Mon23
,
HMP24
]
have used Eldan’s stochastic localization
[
Eld13
,
Eld20
]
method to give sampling algorithms for certain distributions arising in statistical physics.
These works provide an algorithmic implementation for the drift in the diffusion process, which is defined by the score, using approximate message passing and natural gradient descent (see also
[
Cel22
]
).

Finally, in a concurrent and independent work
[
GKL24
]

the authors give diffusion-based algorithms for the special case of learning spherical (identity covariance) Gaussian mixtures, qualitatively matching the best-known results by
[
DK20
]
.
Our focus here is different: we learn Gaussian
mixtures with
general, well-conditioned
covariance matrices
and improve over the prior works
[
MV10
,
BDJ+22
]
yielding exponential savings in runtime
when the number of components
k
is not constant, i.e.,
k=\Omega(\mathrm{polylog}d)
.

Statistical guarantees for score matching

Several recent works have investigated the
statistical
complexity of score matching.
[
KHR23
]
showed a connection between the statistical efficiency of score matching and functional inequalities satisfied by the data distribution.
[
PRS+24
]
studied score matching for learning log-polynomial distributions. Like in
[
KHR23
]
, they focus on the score function of the base distribution and not noisy versions thereof; as the authors note, in this case, score matching is computationally tractable as it is exactly an instance of polynomial regression, and their focus was on proving that the statistical efficiency of score matching here is comparable to that of maximum likelihood estimation.

Recently,
[
WWY24
]
established the optimal rate for score estimation of nonparametric distributions in high dimensions.
[
CHZW23
,
OAS23
]
studied the sample complexity of score matching for nonparametric distributions specifically using a neural network.
[
MW23a
]
bounded the sample complexity of learning certain graphical models using diffusion models by arguing that neural network layers can implement iterations of certain variational inference algorithms. We emphasize once more that these guarantees are all statistical in nature rather than algorithmic.

2
Technical overview

In this section, we provide an overview of our approach, sketches for the main
arguments, and pointers to the relevant sections for more details.

2.1
Learning via DDPM

Our algorithm is based on a denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM)
[
SDWMG15
,
SE19
,
HJA20
]
. Here we give a self-contained exposition of the basic tools from this literature (see
Section

3.2
for details); readers who are familiar with diffusion models may safely skip to
Proposition

2.1
below.

The most common
[
SSDK+20
,
Mon23
]
approach is to consider the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, which
given some distribution
q_{0}
corresponds to the SDE
d\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}dt+\sqrt{2}d\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{0}
. The distribution
q_{0}
here corresponds to the target distribution that we want to learn to generate samples from. In what follows, we use
q_{t}
to denote the law of the OU process at time
t
. It holds that
q_{t}
converges to the standard normal distribution and in particular
at time
t
we have that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}=e^{-t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}+~\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},~~~\text{for}~~~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{0},~~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}\,.

(1)

Given some terminal timestep
T
of the forward OU process with distribution
q_{T}
, the following reverse process perfectly transforms noisy distribution
q_{T}
(which is close to standard Gaussian) to the data distribution
q_{0}
:

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}=\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}+2\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{T-t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t}\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{0}\sim q_{T}\,.

In this reverse process, the iterate
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}
is distributed according to
q_{T-t}
for every
t\in[0,T]
, so that the final iterate
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{T}
is distributed according to the data distribution
q_{0}
. To be able to generate samples using the reverse SDE we need
access to the
score function

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
.
Given approximate oracle access to the score function of the target density
q_{0}
(for us this is the mixture of Gaussians) at close enough noise levels, we can discretize
the reverse SDE that starts with a sample from the Gaussian noise and generates a sample whose distribution is close to the target density.
In particular, for timesteps
t_{0},\ldots,t_{N}
, given estimates
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},T-t_{\ell})

we will be using the following update rule to generate a sample (sometimes called the exponential integrator scheme as it replaces the time-dependent score term in the reverse SDE with the score
approximation at time-step
T-t_{\ell}
). More precisely, at the
\ell
-th iteration,
we sample
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{\ell}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
and update our guess as follows:

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell+1}\leftarrow~\rho_{\ell}~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell}+2(\rho_{\ell}-1)~\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell},T-t_{\ell})+\sqrt{\rho_{\ell}^{2}-1}~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{\ell},

(2)

where
\rho_{\ell}
is an appropriately chosen “step-size” parameter, see
Algorithm

1
for more details. Several recent works (see, e.g.,
[
CCL+23b
,
LLT23
,
CLL22
,
BDBDD23
]
) have studied the convergence of the above (discretized) reverse SDE to the data distribution under black-box assumptions on the quality
of the score estimates
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. We will be using a recent result from
[
BDBDD23
]
(see
Lemma

3.3
) that places minimal assumptions on the data distribution and gives fast convergence rates.
More precisely, for the case of well-conditioned Gaussian mixtures, it implies that if the score functions are approximated within
L_{2}
error roughly

\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon/\tau)
, then iterating
Equation

2
will produce a sample within total variation distance
\varepsilon

from the target Gaussian mixture after
\mathrm{poly}(d\tau/\varepsilon)
iterations.

Learning the score

We have now reduced the original sampling problem to roughly
N=\mathrm{poly}(d\tau/\varepsilon)
regression problems to get the approximate
score functions at times
t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}
.
More precisely for every
t\in\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}\}
we would like to use
some expressive enough class of functions
\mathcal{G}
and
solve the following minimization (score-matching) problem:

\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathcal{G}}\;\;\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})\|^{2}_{2}]

where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
is generated by adding the Gaussian noise
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
to
the sample
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{M}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}=e^{-t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}+\sqrt{1-e^{-2t}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
. Since we have sample access to the unknown mixture
\mathcal{M}
, we can
generate i.i.d. copies of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
to solve the regression task. However, the target score function at noise-level
t
is not available (as it depends on the
density of the unknown mixture). A standard workaround
[
Hyv05
,
Vin11
,
HJA20
,
SSDK+20
]

is the denoising
approach where conditional on the observed
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
we try to predict the added
noise
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
.
It is a well-known consequence of Gaussian integration by parts (see e.g. Appendix A of
[
CCL+23b
]
for a proof) that the following regression task is equivalent to the original score-matching
problem with the benefit that it does not require knowledge of the score function of
the distribution
q_{t}
(that corresponds to the distribution of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
):

\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathcal{G}}\;\;L_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}})=\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathcal{G}}\;\;\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}\Bigl[\Big\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}_{2}\Bigr]

(3)

Our main technical contribution is an efficient algorithm that uses the above denoising formulation of the score-matching problem and yields an approximation
to the score function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})
.

Proposition 2.1

(Informal - Efficiently Learning the Score -
Proposition

7.10
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
\tau
-well-conditioned mixture.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
and noise scale
t\geq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon/\tau)
, there exists an algorithm that draws
d^{\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/\varepsilon)}
samples from
\mathcal{M}
,
runs in sample-polynomial time, and returns a score function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\cdot)
such that with high probability it holds

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{M}_{t}}\big[\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|^{2}\big]\leq\varepsilon.

A detailed theorem statement and the details of the algorithm can be found in
Proposition

7.10
. The details of the proof of
Proposition

2.1
can be found
in
Section

7
.
Combining the above efficient algorithm with the convergence rate of the reverse
SDE we are able to get our end-to-end efficient algorithm for sampling from the
mixture
\mathcal{M}
. Our efficient algorithm in
Proposition

2.1
relies on a structural result showing that the score function of the
mixture
\mathcal{M}
can be approximated by a piecewise-polynomial function,
and an efficient algorithm to recover the partition of the piecewise polynomial approximation. In the following sections, we describe the main ideas of each part.

Remark 2.2

(Learning mixtures of low-dimensional (degenerate) Gaussians)
.

Here we briefly discuss how our techniques can also give a learning guarantee even when the covariances of the components are degenerate. The reason is that we can simply stop the reverse diffusion
\delta
time steps early. Instead of approximately sampling from the original mixture
\mathcal{M}
, this would approximately sample in total variation from a slightly noisy version of
\mathcal{M}
, namely the distribution
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
given by starting at
\mathcal{M}
and running the forward process for a small amount of time
\delta
. Given a component
{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})
of
\mathcal{N}
, the corresponding component of
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
is given by
{\cal N}(e^{-\delta}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},e^{-2\delta}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}+(1-e^{-2\delta})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
. In particular, the minimum singular value of the covariance is at least
1-e^{-2\delta}=\Omega(\delta)
, and we can thus apply
Theorem

1.2
to
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
instead of
\mathcal{M}
, incurring exponential dependence on
\mathrm{poly}(1/\delta)
. Moreover, the Wasserstein distance between
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
scales with
\delta(R+\mathrm{poly}(\beta/\alpha))
. Altogether, we find that we can sample from a distribution that is TV-close to a distribution which is Wasserstein-close to
\mathcal{M}
, even if
\mathcal{M}
might have degenerate covariances.

2.2
Approximating the score function using piecewise polynomials

We now present the key ideas behind our main technical result showing that a piecewise polynomial
approximation of the score function exists. In the following discussion, we will be focusing on
estimating the score function of the Gaussian mixture at a specific noise level
t
. At noise level

t
, each component of the mixture is rescaled by
e^{-t}
and convolved with a mean-zero Gaussian with covariance
(1-e^{-2t})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
(see
Equation

1
). Therefore, the score function at every noise level
corresponds to the score function of a Gaussian mixture with
means
e^{-t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}
and covariances
e^{-2t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}+(1-e^{-2t})~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
, where

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
denote the parameters of
i^{\text{th}}
component of the original target mixture
\mathcal{M}
. For simplicity, we assume that the minimum mixing weight of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
is at least
\mathrm{poly}(1/k)
in the following discussion.
It turns out that the bottleneck is to approximate the score function of the original
mixture
\mathcal{M}
and therefore, to keep the notation simple, for this presentation
we will focus on this problem. We will denote the score
function (i.e., the gradient of the log-density) of a mixture of Gaussians by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})
:

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})=-\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\underbrace{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 11.38109ptw_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\frac{\lambda_{i}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}

(4)

Proposition 2.3

(Informal - Efficient Piecewise Polynomial Approximation -
Proposition

7.9
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
\tau
-well-conditioned mixture of
k
Gaussians.
There exists a function
\mathsf{c}(\cdot):\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto[{n_{\rm c}}]
and polynomials

p_{1},\ldots,p_{{n_{\rm c}}}
of degree at most
\ell=\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/\varepsilon)
such that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-p_{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|^{2}]\leq\varepsilon\ ,

Moreover, there exists an efficient algorithm that with high-probability
finds this piecewise polynomial approximation with
d^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell)}
samples
and runtime.

Why piecewise polynomials?

We first give some intuition behind the structure of the score function of a Gaussian mixture, and its piecewise polynomial approximation.
We observe that the score function (see
Equation

4
) is a weighted combination of linear functions. For example, for a mixture of two standard
one-dimensional Gaussians with means at
-R
and
R
, it behaves (approximately)
like the function
-\mathds{1}\{x\leq 0\}(x+R)-\mathds{1}\{x\geq 0\}(x-R)
, see the left figure in
Figure

1
.
We observe that the total length of support of the mixture is roughly an interval of length
O(R)

and the slope of the score function is approximately
O(R)
close to the origin.
We would like to have a polynomial approximation
of degree
\mathrm{poly}(\log R/\varepsilon)
for this instance but naively applying polynomial approximation results (see, e.g., Jackson’s theorem,
Lemma

7.2
) would yield a degree
\mathrm{poly}(R/\varepsilon)
even for 1-dimensional mixtures.
1
1

1

When dealing with
d
dimensional mixtures things are even worse since the effective support has a radius depending on the dimension
d
.

Therefore, as we observe in
Figure

1
, two reasons prohibit us from applying polynomial approximation results in a black-box manner: (1)
the total support is of radius
R
and (2) there are regions (far from the mixture means) where the
slope of the score function is also large (also
R
).

For the case of two Gaussians, we see that the “effective” support is much smaller
(intervals of size roughly
\sqrt{\log(1/\varepsilon)}
around the means). Moreover, by focusing on
the “effective” support we also avoid the area where the derivative of the score function is
large (close to the origin). Thus one could hope to solve both issues discussed above by
creating an interpolating polynomial by concentrating the nodes on the effective support.
Such an approach would work when the support consisted of actual “hard” intervals (and not “approximate” intervals with Gaussian tails). The main issue is a race condition
between the value of the interpolating polynomial far from the interpolation nodes (roughly exponentially large in the degree) and the decay of the Gaussian density.
While this race condition can be solved in some special cases (such as for mixtures of two Gaussians
with very well-separated means on
-R
and
+R
), in general when more Gaussians are present in
the mixture, the mental image of a union of “hard” intervals is incorrect
and it is not clear that the tails will always be able to cancel out the large error of the polynomial far from the interpolation intervals.

The above structure of the score function naturally leads to a piecewise polynomial approximation
approach. For the symmetric mixture of two Gaussians discussed above there is an obvious candidate
for the partition: we should perform polynomial approximation in
\mathrm{poly}(\log(R/\varepsilon))
sized intervals around
\pm R
and output zero in the rest of the space. That would lead to the desired degree of

\mathrm{poly}(\log(R/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon)
.
For the more complicated example of the right figure of
Figure

1
we could similarly try to split the instance in
an interval containing almost all the mass of two left components and one interval containing the
three right components and perform polynomial approximation (and output zero out of those two intervals).
In both examples, by using the piecewise polynomial approximation we avoided both issues discussed earlier, i.e., using polynomial approximation over large intervals or approximating over intervals where the derivative of the score is large.

Clustering and polynomial approximation: a win-win analysis

Piecewise polynomial regression is a computationally hard, non-convex problem
when we search both for the polynomials and for the partition of the space.
Therefore, we have to make sure that we have an efficient algorithm to find the partition of the space and then apply polynomial regression inside each cell of the partition. Our main algorithm
is enabled by a win-win argument in the sense that the areas where polynomial approximation requires
high degree (i.e.,
\mathrm{poly}(R)
) can be easily avoided by a crude clustering algorithm and the
areas where the clustering algorithm fails to separate between a set of components of the mixture are those where the polynomial approximation
is effective.

Figure 1:
When approximation is hard, clustering is easy. On the left figure,
we plot the density (gold) and score function (blue) of mixtures of two standard Gaussians with well-separated means (their distance is
R
).
We observe that in that case, the score function
is (almost) a piecewise linear function with a large slope, i.e., roughly
R
,
close to the origin. In the right image, we have a mixture of
5
Gaussians with different means and variances
that can be split into two clusters: a group of
2
on the left and
3
on the right. Again the area where
the derivative of the score function (blue) is high, falls in between the two clusters (where the Gaussian density is exponentially small). In both cases, a piecewise polynomial approximation yields the correct degree
that scaling with
(\log R)/\varepsilon
instead of
R/\varepsilon
. Moreover, we expect that it is easy to cluster
the points in the corresponding sub-mixtures that have much smaller effective support than the original
mixture.

2.3
Approximating the score given a crude partition

As we observed in the previous examples, the main difficulty in providing a polynomial approximation of the score function arises when it involves multiple Gaussians that are far apart. We first make
more precise the notion of ‘‘crude’’ clustering
2
2

2

We use the terms “clustering” and “partition” function interchangeably.
that we require.

Definition 2.4

(
(\Delta_{\rm in},\Delta_{\rm out})
-separated partition)
.

Given a mixture of Gaussians

\mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k})
,
we require that the clustering function
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
assigns
each
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
to one of
n_{c}
subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n_{c}}
of
[k]

that form a partition of the original
k
components such that:

1.

If
\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j}
belong in different subsets
U_{t}

and
U_{t^{\prime}}
, they have to be
at least

\Delta_{\rm out}=\mathrm{poly}(\tau k\log(1/\varepsilon))
far in parameter distance, i.e.,

D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}\geq\Delta_{\rm out}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j}
belong in the same subset
U_{t}
, they have to be
at most

\Delta_{\rm in}=\mathrm{poly}(\tau k\log(1/\varepsilon))
far in parameter distance, i.e.,

D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
.

3.

\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
is consistent with the partition
U_{1},\ldots,U_{t}
with high-probability, i.e.,
for any
i\in U_{t}
,
\pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\neq t]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{part}}
, where
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{part}}
is a small error parameter.

Given the above
(\Delta_{\rm in},\Delta_{\rm out})
-partition, our proof consists of two steps:
(i) show that we can reduce the original problem of approximating the score function of the whole mixture to approximating the score function of the sub-mixtures
U_{t}
and (ii)
providing low-degree approximations of the sub-mixture score functions. We describe
these steps in the next two paragraphs.

Simplifying the score

As we discussed, the first obstacle in approximating the score function is that
it is a function over a domain of radius
\mathrm{poly}(R)
(inducing a
\mathrm{poly}(R)
dependency on the degree).
Fortunately, there is an additional structure connecting the weights
w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
the linear terms
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
. We use this structure to prove that when
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
is sampled
from some component
\mathcal{N}_{i}
then on expectation over the component
\mathcal{N}_{i}
we can remove
a term in the score function corresponding to a component
\mathcal{N}_{j}
that is far
from
\mathcal{N}_{i}
without introducing large error, see
Lemma

6.3
.
More precisely, we show that given a partition function
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
that satisfies

Definition

2.4
, for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
where
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t
,
we can “simplify” the score function by removing
the contribution of all components
\mathcal{N}_{j}
that do not belong in
U_{t}
.

Given a subset
U_{t}
of indices of
[k]
, we denote by

\mathcal{M}(U_{t})
the submixture containing the components
\mathcal{N}_{i}
for
i\in U_{t}
and by

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
the score function containing only the contribution of
components from
U_{t}
, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))=-\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\frac{\lambda_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\sum_{j\in U_{t}}\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}

We prove the following proposition showing that,
inside each cell
t
of the partition given by
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
, we can replace the original score function
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})
by the score function of the
sub-mixture
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
.
Each sub-mixture score function corresponding to
U_{t}
contains components that are all

\Delta_{\rm in}
-close to each other, thus reducing the effective radius of the approximation domain to
\mathrm{poly}(\log R)
.

Proposition 2.5

(Informal – Score Simplification, see
Proposition

6.1
)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. let
\mathcal{M}
be a
\tau
-well-conditioned mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions and satisfies
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{F}\leq R
for all the components.
Moreover, assume that
\mathsf{c}
satisfies
Definition

2.4
.
Define the following piecewise approximation to the score function

s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\,.

It holds that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\mathrm{poly}(k\tau R)\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Polynomial approximation of the simplified score

Recall from Eq. (
4
) that the score function for any Gaussian mixture is a sum of the softmax function
w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
multiplied by a linear function
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})
. A polynomial approximation of the softmax will provide a polynomial approximation for the simplified score. Note that we want to approximate the simplified score with the degree at most
\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/\varepsilon)
to obtain runtime of polynomial regression of
O(d^{\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/\varepsilon)})
.

The degree of a polynomial approximation of a function generally depends on the domain of the approximation and smoothness of the function (in terms of the norm of its gradient), see
Lemma

7.2
. The softmax function is smooth and has a bounded gradient but the input to the softmax is
\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}}\}_{i=1}^{|U_{t}|}
which can be as large as
\mathrm{poly}(d)
and hence, the degree of the naive polynomial approximation could be
\mathrm{poly}(d/\varepsilon)
.

To overcome this issue, we show that even though each input
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}}^{2}
is large, there exists a normalization of the softmax for which the inputs to the softmax are
\mathrm{poly}(\tau\Delta_{\rm in})
. More precisely, we normalize the softmax such that
\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\rangle\}_{i=1}^{|U_{t}|}
are the inputs to the softmax function and show that its norm is
\mathrm{poly}(\tau\Delta_{\rm in})
with high probability. Therefore, using multivariate Jackson’s theorem (
Lemma

7.2
), we obtain the polynomial approximation for the softmax function and hence, for the simplified score function.

Lemma 2.6

(Informal - See
Lemma

7.6
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(U)
be a
\tau
-well-conditioned mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions restricted to the subset of components in
U
. Then, there exist a polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
of degree
\mathrm{poly}(\tau\Delta_{\rm in}/\varepsilon)
and coefficients bounded in magnitude by
dR\exp(\mathrm{poly}(\tau\Delta_{\rm in}/\varepsilon))
such that for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, with high probability, the polynomial satisfies

\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|\leq\varepsilon.

2.4
Crude clustering via PCA

We now describe our crude clustering algorithm for obtaining the partition satisfying
the assumptions of
Definition

2.4
. Our approach consists of two main steps: (1)
approximately recover the span of the means and covariances using PCA on the second and
fourth-order moment tensors of the mixture and (2) recover estimates of the parameters by
brute forcing over the
k
-dimensional subspace recovered in the first step and using
pairwise log-likelihood tests to create the final partition function.

Obtaining estimates of means and covariances

The algorithm operates in two phases. First, we obtain a crude estimate for the subspace spanned by the means, after which we brute-force within this low-dimensional subspace to find points close to each of the means. Second, we use these mean estimates to form an estimator for the subspace spanned by the covariances, after which we can similarly brute-force to find points close to each of the covariances.
With roughly
d^{O(k)}
runtime, we can construct a list of candidate parameters for the means
and covariances of the mixture containing crude (in the sense that they can be
\mathrm{poly}(k\tau)
-far) of the target parameters.

Lemma 2.7

(Informal – Recovering crude estimates of the parameters, see
Lemma

4.1
)
.

There is an algorithm that returns a list
\mathcal{W}
such that for every
i\in[k]
, there exists
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i})\in\mathcal{W}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\|^{2}\lesssim\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}\|_{F}\lesssim k^{3/2}\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}+k^{2}\alpha\log R
. Furthermore,
|\mathcal{W}|\leq(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k^{2})}\cdot d^{O(k)}
, and the algorithm runs in time
(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k^{2})}\cdot(\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)+d^{O(k)})
and draws
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)
samples.

We use PCA on the covariance of the mixture
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal}]

to obtain the subspace spanned by the means. We observe that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}^{\intercal}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
.
The main idea here is to think of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}
as approximately low-rank and treat the contribution
of the covariances as an error
{\cal E}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
.
Since the covariance matrices
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
are well-conditioned (i.e., their eigenvalues are not bigger
than
\beta
(see
Definition

1.1
) we can show that if some
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}

is larger than
\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
then its contribution in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}
cannot be “hidden” by the error term
{\cal E}
and will have a large projection onto the subspace spanned by the top eigenvectors
of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}
. The proof of this claim follows by a standard argument for
k
-SVD and can be found in

Section

4.1
.

Finding estimates for the covariances is more complicated but similarly relies on recovering the subspace
spanned by the low-rank components of the (flattened) fourth-order tensor

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Psi}}=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal})\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal})]\,.

The intuition behind our approach is that if the means of the mixture were all sufficiently close to zero, then the top-
k
singular subspace of the matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Psi}}

can be shown to contain points close to
\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}),\ldots,\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k})
. In general, if the means are arbitrary, then we can use the estimates
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k}
derived in the previous section to approximately “recenter” the mixture components near zero. Since the means recovered in the previous step were already
crude
\mathrm{poly}(k)
approximations of the true means a careful error analysis must be done so that this
recentering does not introduce significantly more error (i.e., depending on the dimension
d
) in the covariance
estimates. We refer to
Section

4
and
Algorithm

3
for more details.

Clustering using the log-likelihood ratios

We now present our main clustering guarantee, which leverages the estimates for the parameters we obtained previously. As those estimates are only crude approximations to the true parameters, we will obtain a commensurately crude clustering.

Our algorithm starts by brute-forcing over mean-based and covariance-based partitions
{\cal S}
(resp.
{\cal T}
).

{\cal S}
(resp.
{\cal T}
) partitions the mixture components into groups such that any two components in the same group have means (resp. covariances) that are not far, and any two components from two different groups have means (resp. covariances) that are not close. Their common refinement is a partition
{\cal U}
satisfying
the assumptions of
Definition

2.4
: any two components in the same group have both means and covariances not too far, and any two components from two different groups either have means not too close or covariances not too close.

By brute-forcing over pairs of partitions of
[k]
(of which there are at most
k^{2k}
) we may assume we have access to
{\cal S}
and
{\cal T}
, and thus to
{\cal U}
. Our goal is then to assign to every
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
an index into the partition
{\cal U}
. For
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
which is sampled from the
i
-th component of the mixture which belongs to the
t
-th group in
{\cal U}
, we would like our assignment to be
t
with high probability.
At a high level, the idea is as follows. It is not too hard to determine which group in
{\cal S}
a given point
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
should belong to, simply by checking which mean estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}
is closest to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
after projecting to the subspace spanned by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k}
. For each group in
{\cal S}
, we can then effectively restrict our attention to components within that group and focus on clustering them according to their covariances. Roughly speaking, we accomplish this by comparing log-likelihoods of sampling
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
under
{\cal N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1}),\ldots,{\cal N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{k})
and choosing the group in
{\cal T}
containing the component maximizing log-likelihood. For more details, we refer to
Section

5
and to
Proposition

5.2
for the formal clustering statement that we prove.

2.5
Avoiding the doubly exponential dependency on
k

Here we provide some intuition for the origin of the doubly exponential dependence on
k
which is implicit in existing works on learning mixtures of general Gaussians with the method of moments, and how our technique outlined above avoids this issue. Our starting point is the algorithm of
[
MV10
]
; in fact, for this discussion, it will suffice to consider the case of
d=1
and components of variance 1.

Specialized to this case, in the analysis in
[
MV10
]
, the authors first proved that if all of the components have means with nonnegligible separation, say
\eta
, from each other, then one can learn the means by brute-forcing over a grid with sufficiently small granularity and finding a setting of parameters in this grid for which the corresponding mixture matches the first
O(k)
moments with the target mixture to error
\eta^{k}
(here we ignore constants in the exponent for simplicity).

Now what happens if the minimal separation
\eta
is arbitrarily small? The authors noted that for means that are particularly close, one can simply “merge them”: they are statistically close to a single component, and in a bounded number of samples one would not be able to tell the difference. Because the number of samples used by the algorithm outlined above is
(1/\eta)^{k}
, this implies that if there is some scale
\eta
at which there is a
gap
in the sense that all means are either
\eta^{k}
-close or
\eta
-far apart, then one can learn in the same amount of time/samples as in the
\eta
-separated case.

The last question that remains is how to ensure such a scale exists. The idea is that if one looks at
k^{2}+1
consecutive windows
\{[\eta^{k^{i}},\eta^{k^{i-1}}]\}_{i=1,\ldots,k^{2}+1}
, by pigeonhole principle there must exist some window such that the separation between any pair of means lies outside this window. At that scale, one can apply the above reasoning to learn the means. This is the origin of the doubly exponential scaling in
k
that is present in all existing algorithms for learning mixtures of general Gaussians, including the state-of-the-art guarantee of
[
BDJ+22
]
.

It is instructive to contrast this with our approach. The main reason for the doubly exponential dependence in the above windowing argument was that one needed a scale at which the components break up into “gapped clusters” such that the separation within clusters is significantly smaller than the separation across clusters. For this clustering structure to exist, we need to go down potentially to a doubly exponentially small scale. In contrast, in our work, we make do with a very crude clustering for the purposes of our piecewise regression. We simply require that for components from different clusters, their parameter distance is sufficiently large, while for components from the same cluster, their parameter distance is
not too large
. Crucially, we don’t need to make any assumption about a gap between the intra- versus inter-cluster separations, ensuring we avoid the doubly exponential dependence on
k
.

3
Diffusion models and other technical preliminaries

In this section, we collect various technical ingredients. The bulk of this section is dedicated to an exposition of diffusion models in
Section

3.2
.

3.1
Notation for mixture models

Throughout the paper, we use either
q
or
q_{0}
to denote the data distribution on
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e., the mixture of Gaussians with means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}
, covariances
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k}
, and mixing weights
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
respectively. We will use
\mathcal{N}_{i}
to denote the distribution for its
i
-th component, i.e.
{\cal N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})
. We use
p
or
p_{T}
to denote the learned distribution.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})
be a
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned Gaussian mixture.
We say that a partition of
[k]
into subsets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}
is
(\Delta_{\rm in},\Delta_{\rm out})
-separated
if for all
i,j\in S_{\ell}
it holds that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
and for all
i\in S_{\ell}
,
j\in S_{\ell^{\prime}}

for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
it holds
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}\geq\Delta_{\rm out}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}(S_{i})
the mixture distribution corresponding to the components of
S_{i}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}(S_{i})=\frac{1}{|S_{i}|}\sum_{j\in S_{i}}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j})
.

Moreover, given a mixture
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\mathcal{D}_{i}
we denote by
\mathcal{M}^{J}
the joint distribution over tuples

(j,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
where
j=i
with probability
\lambda_{i}
and, conditional on
j=i
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
is drawn from
\mathcal{D}_{i}
.

3.2
Learning Gaussian mixtures via a denoising diffusion process

We start by introducing some standard terminology and notation
on diffusion models. We will be using the diffusion algorithmic template in a more or less
black box manner and therefore we try to keep the presentation short but still self-contained.
Throughout the paper, we use either
q
or
q_{0}
to denote the data distribution on
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
. The two main components in diffusion models are the
forward process
and the
reverse process
. The forward process transforms samples from the data distribution into noise, for instance via the
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) process
:

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}=-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t}\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{0}\,,

where
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a standard Brownian motion in
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
.
We use
q_{t}
to denote the law of the OU process at time
t
. Note that for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\sim q_{t}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}=\exp(-t)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}+\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{0},\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})\,.

(5)

The reverse process then transforms noise into samples, thus performing generative modeling. Ideally, this could be achieved by running the following stochastic differential equation for some choice of terminal time
T
:

\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}=\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}+2\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\ln q_{T-t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{t})\}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t}\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{0}\sim q_{T}\,,

(6)

where now
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{t}
is the reversed Brownian motion. In this reverse process, the iterate
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}^{\leftarrow}_{t}
is distributed according to
q_{T-t}
for every
t\in[0,T]
, so that the final iterate
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\leftarrow}_{T}
is distributed according to the data distribution
q_{0}
.
The function
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\ln q_{t}
is called the
score function
and is required so that
we are able to run the reverse SDE and generate samples from the unknown distribution. Ideally, we would like to have access to an approximate
oracle
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
it is a good approximation to the score function
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
:

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\sim q_{t}}[\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\ln q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}_{t}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})\|^{2}]\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\,.

(7)

To obtain such a function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
, one would an expressive enough set of candidate functions
\mathcal{G}
and then try to optimize the score matching loss:

\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}\in\mathcal{G}}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\sim q_{t}}[\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\ln q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|^{2}]\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}\,.

However, as the density function of
q_{t}
is unknown the above minimization
problem cannot be solved directly.
A standard calculation (see e.g. Appendix A of
[
CCL+23b
]
) shows that this is equivalent to minimizing the
DDPM objective
in which one wants to predict the noise
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
from the noisy observation
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
, i.e.

\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathcal{G}}\;\;L_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t})=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}\Biggl[\Big\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\Biggr]\,.

(8)

In this work we focus specifically on the optimization problem (
8
) and show that it can be solved efficiently
when the underlying target density
q_{0}
is a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions.

Algorithm 1

GenerateSample

Input:

Score estimation error
\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}
, confidence
\delta
,
sequence of time steps
t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}

Output:

A sample
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{N}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}

1

for

\ell\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}

do

2

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\cdot,T-t_{\ell})\leftarrow\textsc{LearnScore}(t_{\ell},\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}},\delta)

\triangleright
Learn the score function at all time steps

3

end for

4

for

\ell\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}

do

5

Set
\rho_{\ell}=e^{(t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell})/2}

6

Sample
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{\ell}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})

7

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell+1}\leftarrow~\rho_{\ell}~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell}+2(\rho_{\ell}-1)~\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{\ell},T-t_{\ell})+\sqrt{\rho_{\ell}^{2}-1}~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{\ell}

\triangleright
Run the (discretized) reverse SDE

8

end for

9

return

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{N}

We are now ready to present and prove our main result: an efficient algorithm for learning well-conditioned
GMMs.

Theorem 3.2

(Efficient Sampler for GMMs)
.

Fix
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and let
\mathcal{M}
be an
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned mixture of
k
Gaussians. Let
\tau=(\beta/\alpha)\,\log R
,

\varepsilon_{\text{score}}=\varepsilon/\log(R/(\alpha\varepsilon))
,
\delta=\alpha\varepsilon/R
, and let
time sequence
t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}
be as defined in
Lemma

3.3
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta_{\text{f}}
,

Algorithm

1
draws

M=d^{\mathrm{poly}(k\tau/(\lambda_{\rm min}\varepsilon))}\log\frac{1}{\delta_{\text{f}}}
samples from
\mathcal{M}
,
runs in sample-polynomial time, and generates a sample

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{N}
whose distribution is
\varepsilon
-close in total variation to
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

We are going to
use the following result on the convergence of the discretized reverse SDE with the score
approximation that we use in
Algorithm

1
.

Lemma 3.3

(Convergence given approximate scores,
[
BDBDD23
]
)
.

Fix some
\delta\in(0,1)
,
T\geq 1
and let
N
be some even integer larger
than
\log(1/\delta)
and let
\kappa>0
be larger than a sufficiently large
constant multiple of
(T+\log(1/\delta))/N
.
Set
t_{0}=0
,
t_{N/2}=T-1
,
t_{N}=T-\delta
.
Moreover, set
t_{1},\ldots,t_{N/2-1}
equally spaced on
[0,T-1]
, i.e.,

t_{\ell+1}-t_{\ell}=\kappa>0
for all
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,N/2-1\}

and
T-t_{N/2+1},\ldots T-t_{N-1}
exponentially decaying, i.e.,

t_{N/2+\ell+1}-t_{N/2+\ell}=\kappa/(1+\kappa)^{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,N/2-2\}
and
{}_{\ell}\leq\kappa\min(1,T-t_{\ell})
. Assume that the data distribution and the score function satisfy the following assumptions.

1.

\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}{}_{\ell}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim q_{t_{\ell}}}[\|\nabla\log q_{T-t_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},T-t_{\ell})\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}
.

2.

The target distribution
q_{0}
on
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
has finite second moment.

For any
t\in[0,T]
denote by
q_{t}
the distribution of
\exp(-t)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}+\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0}\sim q_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})

and denote by
p_{t_{N}}
the distribution of the output
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{N}
of
Algorithm

1
.
It holds that

\mathrm{KL}(q_{\delta}\|p_{t_{N}})\lesssim\epsilon_{\mathrm{score}}^{2}+\kappa^{2}dN+\kappa dT+\mathrm{KL}(q_{T}\|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}))\,.

We first show that the guarantee of
Lemma

3.3
yields
a total variation bound between
p_{t_{N}}
and the target Gaussian mixture
\mathcal{M}
. By Pinsker’s inequality, we obtain that

\mathrm{TV}(p_{t_{N}},q_{\delta})\lesssim\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}(q_{\delta}\|p_{t_{N}})}
.
Moreover, by a triangle inequality, we obtain that

\mathrm{TV}(q_{0},p_{t_{N}})\leq\mathrm{TV}(p_{t_{N}},q_{\delta})+\mathrm{TV}(q_{\delta},q_{0})
. Therefore, we have to control
\mathrm{TV}(q_{0},q_{\delta})
. Using again Pinsker’s inequality we obtain that

\mathrm{TV}(q_{0},q_{\delta})\lesssim\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}(q_{0}\|q_{\delta})}
.
To control the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the target

q_{0}
that corresponds to the well-conditioned mixture and

q_{\delta}
. We observe that
q_{\delta}
is also a Gaussian mixture
with parameters
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\exp(-\delta)
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}e^{-2\delta}+(1-e^{-2\delta})~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
. We denote this mixture by
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
Since
\mathrm{KL}
is convex we obtain
that

\mathrm{KL}(q_{\delta}\|q_{0})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\mathrm{KL}(\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})\|\mathcal{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}))\leq\max_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{KL}(\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})\|\mathcal{N}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}))\,.

We can now use the following standard bound for the Kullback-Leibler distance
between two Normal distributions

\mathrm{KL}(\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}))\lesssim\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2})\|_{2}^{2}
. We have that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}e^{-\delta})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{R^{2}}{\alpha}(1-e^{-\delta})^{2}\leq\frac{R^{2}}{\alpha}~\delta^{2}\,,

where the last inequality follows by the fact that
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
and the fact that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{2}\leq R

and the inequality
e^{x}\geq x+1
. Moreover, if
s_{1},\ldots s_{d}
are the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}
, we have that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Big(1-\frac{s_{i}e^{-2\delta}+(1-e^{-2\delta})}{s_{i}}\Big)^{2}=(1-e^{-2\delta})^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{1-s_{i}}{s_{i}}\Big)^{2}\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}R^{2}\,,

where the last inequality follows by the assumption that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim R
and the fact that
s_{i}\geq\alpha
for all
i
.
Putting the above together, we obtain that

\mathrm{TV}(p_{t_{N}},\mathcal{M})\lesssim\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}(q_{\delta}\|p_{t_{N}})}+\delta R/\alpha
.

Similarly, we have to control the convergence error of the forward OU process
\mathrm{KL}(q_{T}\|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}))
. Similarly to the above argument,
by the convexity of the Kullback-Leibler, we obtain that it suffices to control
the KL divergence between any component of the mixture and the standard normal
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
. Using the same bound for the KL divergence as above, we have that

\mathrm{KL}(q_{T}\|\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{0}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})\leq\max_{i=1}^{k}(e^{-2T}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}+e^{-4T}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{F}^{2})\lesssim e^{-2T}R^{2}\,.

To make the forward process converge to an
\varepsilon
-approximate Gaussian, we take
T=\log(R/\varepsilon)
. We choose
\varepsilon_{\text{score}}=\varepsilon/\log(R/(\alpha\varepsilon))
and
\delta=\alpha\varepsilon/R
. Additionally, we have
{}_{\ell}\leq\kappa
for all
\ell
. Therefore, we have

\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}{}_{\ell}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim q_{t_{\ell}}}[\|\nabla\log q_{T-t_{\ell}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},T-t_{\ell})\|_{2}^{2}]\leq\varepsilon\;.

The above choice also yields
\kappa^{2}dN\lesssim(\log^{2}(R/\alpha\varepsilon)d)/N
and
\kappa dT\lesssim(\log^{2}(R/\alpha\varepsilon)d)/N
. Choosing
N=(\log^{2}(R/\alpha\varepsilon)d)/\varepsilon
and combining all the terms in
Lemma

3.3
, we obtain that
\mathrm{TV}(p_{t_{N}},\mathcal{M})\leq\varepsilon
. We obtain sample complexity and runtime of the algorithm by putting
\varepsilon_{\text{score}}=\varepsilon/\log(R/(\alpha\varepsilon))
and failure probability
\delta_{f}=\delta/N
in
Proposition

7.10
.
∎

4
Obtaining crude estimates for the parameters

In this section, we prove the next lemma showing that we can construct a list of candidates for the unknown parameters of the mixture, containing “crude” approximation to the true target parameters.

Lemma 4.1
.

There is an algorithm CrudeEstimate(
q
) which returns a list
\mathcal{W}
such that for every
i\in[k]
, there exists
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i})\in\mathcal{W}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\|^{2}\lesssim\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}\|_{F}\lesssim k^{3/2}\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}+k^{2}\alpha\log R
. Furthermore,
|\mathcal{W}|\leq(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k^{2})}\cdot d^{O(k)}
, and the algorithm runs in time
(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k^{2})}\cdot(\mathrm{poly}(d,1/\beta)+d^{O(k)})
and draws
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)
samples.

The algorithm operates in two phases. First, we obtain a crude estimate for the subspace spanned by the means, after which we brute-force within this subspace to find points close to each of the means. Second, we use these mean estimates to form an estimator for the subspace spanned by the covariances, after which we can similarly brute-force to find points close to each of the covariances.

4.1
Estimating the means

This phase is straightforward: we simply take the top-
k
singular subspace of the empirical second moment matrix (see
Algorithm

2
below).

Lemma 4.2
.

There is an algorithm
CrudeEstimateMeans
(
q
) which returns a list
\mathcal{W}
such that for each
i\in[k]
, there exists
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\|^{2}\lesssim\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
. Furthermore,
|\mathcal{W}|\leq(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k)}
, and the algorithm runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)+(R/\sqrt{\beta})^{O(k)}
and draws
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)
samples.

Algorithm 2

CrudeEstimateMeans(
q
)

Input:

Sample access to
q

Output:

List
\mathcal{W}
containing approximations to
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}

1

Draw samples
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{N}
from
q
for
N\leftarrow\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)

2

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}\leftarrow\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{i}^{\intercal}

3

\widehat{V}\leftarrow
top-
k
singular subspace of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}

4

\mathcal{W}\leftarrow
a
\beta^{1/2}
-net over vectors in
\widehat{V}
with
L_{2}
norm at most
2R

5

return

\mathcal{W}

The analysis (as well as subsequent parts of our proof) uses the following standard bound for
k
-SVD:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}=\sum^{k}_{i=1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}^{\intercal}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}
for
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}\|_{\rm op}\leq\epsilon
. The top-
k
singular subspace of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}
contains vectors
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}_{k}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}_{i}\|^{2}\leq 2\epsilon
for all
i\in[k]
.

Proof.

Define
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}^{*}\triangleq\sum^{k}_{i=1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}^{\intercal}
. Let
\Pi^{\perp}
denote the projector to the orthogonal complement of the top-
k
singular subspace of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}
, and define
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\triangleq\Pi^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}
. Then

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}=\sigma_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}})\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\|^{2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\leq 2\epsilon\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\|^{2}\,,

where in the last step we used Weyl’s inequality to bound
\sigma_{k+1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}})
.

On the other hand,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}=\sum_{j}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{j}\rangle^{2}\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\rangle^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\|^{4}\,,

so we conclude that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\|^{2}\leq 2\epsilon
. If we define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}_{i}=\Pi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}
, where
\Pi
is the projector to the top-
k
singular subspace of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}
, then
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\|^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}\|^{2}\leq 2\epsilon
as claimed.
∎

We now show that the empirical second moment matrix can be used to extract a rough approximation to the span of the means:

Lemma 4.4
.

For
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}
, let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}\triangleq\E[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal}]
. Given
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim\beta
, let
\widehat{V}
denote the top-
k
singular subspace of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}
. Then for every
i\in[k]
, there exists
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\in\widehat{V}
for which
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|^{2}\lesssim\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
.

Proof.

Define
{\cal E}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}^{*}\triangleq\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\intercal}
. We have that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}^{*}+{\cal E}\,,

and
\|{\cal E}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim\beta
.

By
Lemma

4.3
, where we take
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{E}}
therein to be
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}
and
{\cal E}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}
, we find that
\widehat{V}
contains vectors
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\prime}_{k}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\prime}_{i}-\sqrt{\lambda}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|^{2}\lesssim\beta
. So if we take
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\prime}_{i}/\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
, the claimed bound follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

By standard matrix concentration (see, e.g.,
[
Ver18
]
)
with
N=\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)
samples (as set in
Algorithm

2
) we have that the matrix
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}
constructed therein satisfies
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}\|_{\rm op}\leq\beta
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}\triangleq\E_{\mathcal{M}}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal}]=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}^{\intercal}+\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
. We have
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{M}}}-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}^{\intercal}\|_{\rm op}\leq 2\beta
, so by
Lemma

4.4
, the
\beta
-net constructed in
Algorithm

2
contains points which are
O(\beta/\lambda_{\rm min})
-close to each of the means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}
as claimed.
∎

4.2
Estimating the covariances

Next, we show how to recover a rough approximation to the span of the covariance matrices and, as a consequence, produce a net containing rough approximations to each of the covariance matrices. The algorithm is summarized in
Algorithm

3
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\|^{2}\leq\upsilon_{\rm mean}
for all
i\in[k]
. Then there is an algorithm
CrudeEstimateCovariances
(
q,\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\}
) which returns a list
\mathcal{W}
such that for each
i\in[k]
, there exists
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}\|_{F}\lesssim\beta^{1/2}\upsilon_{\rm mean}^{1/2}+k^{3/2}\upsilon_{\rm mean}+k^{5/2}\beta+k^{2}\alpha\log R
.
Furthermore
|\mathcal{W}|\leq d^{O(k)}
, and the algorithm runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)+d^{O(k)}
and draws
\mathrm{poly}(dR/\beta)
samples.

The intuition behind our approach is that if the means of the mixture were all sufficiently close to zero, then the top-
k
singular subspace of the matrix
\E[\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal})\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}^{\intercal})^{\intercal}]
can be shown to contain points close to
\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}),\ldots,\mathrm{vec}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k})
. In general, if the means are arbitrary, then we can use the estimates
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k}
derived in the previous section to approximately “recenter” the mixture components near zero. We now make this intuition precise.

Proof preliminaries.

Define

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\triangleq\mathrm{span}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k})\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}^{\perp}\triangleq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\,.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\parallel}_{i}\triangleq\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\perp}_{i}\triangleq\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}
. Note that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\perp}_{i}\|^{2}=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}^{\perp}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{2}\leq\upsilon_{\rm mean}\,.

Also define

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\zeta}}_{i}\triangleq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}^{\parallel}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}

and note that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\zeta}}_{i}\|^{2}=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{2}\lesssim\upsilon_{\rm mean}\,.

Define
\Delta\geq 1
by

\Delta\triangleq C(\sqrt{\upsilon_{\rm mean}}+\sqrt{k\beta}+k^{1/4}\sqrt{\alpha\log R})

(9)

for sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
. Given
i\in[k]
, define

S_{\rm far}[i]\triangleq\{j\in[k]:\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|\geq\Delta\}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ S_{\rm close}[i]\triangleq\{j\in[k]:\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|\leq\Delta\}\,.

The algorithm we give in this section (
Algorithm

3
) does not require knowledge of
S_{\rm far}[i],S_{\rm close}[i]
; these sets are only defined here for the purpose of analysis.

To approximately “recenter” the mixture components around zero, we will subtract from each sample the mean estimate which is closest to it in the subspace given by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}
. Formally, given
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
, define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\triangleq\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\ \ \ \text{for}\ \ \ i=\argmin_{j\in[k]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|\,.

(10)

For every
i\in[k]
, define

\mathcal{K}_{i}\triangleq\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}:\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}\}\,,

i.e. the set of points which are closest to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}
in the subspace given by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}
.

Finally, given
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
, define

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Psi}}_{00}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})\triangleq\mathrm{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}})\mathrm{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}})^{\intercal}

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Psi}}_{01}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})\triangleq\mathrm{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}^{\perp})\mathrm{vec}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}^{\perp})^{\intercal}

(11)
