\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a]^{-1}\cdot\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=b^{\prime}]\leq 2\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=b^{\prime}]\,,

where we used
Lemma

5.6
and the fact
k\cdot\exp(-\Omega((\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out})^{2}/\alpha\sqrt{k}))\leq 1/2
. Note that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=b^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in S_{a}}\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}}\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=b^{\prime}]

(33)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i^{\prime}\in S_{a}}\sum_{j\in T_{b^{\prime}}}\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})<t_{jj^{\prime}}-\eta\ \forall\ j^{\prime}\in[k]]

(34)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i^{\prime}\in S_{a}}\sum_{j\in T_{b^{\prime}}}\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})<t_{ji}-\eta]

(35)

\displaystyle\leq k^{2}\exp(-\Omega(c^{\prime 2}(\alpha^{4}/\beta^{6})\cdot\min_{j\in T_{b^{\prime}}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\|^{2}_{F}\cdot\min(1,\alpha^{2}/\upsilon_{\rm cov}^{2})))\,,

(36)

where in the last step we used
Lemma

5.4
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Suppose that

\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}\geq C\max(\upsilon_{\rm cov}\beta^{2}/\alpha^{2},c^{2/3}(\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out})^{2/3}\alpha^{1/3},(\beta/\alpha)^{3}\upsilon_{\rm cov})

(37)

for sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
.
Then for any
i\in S_{a}\cap T_{b}
, we have that

\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=0\mid\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a]\leq 2k^{3}\exp(-\Omega(\alpha^{2}\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}/\beta^{3}))\,.

Proof.

We can rewrite the conditional probability as

\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a]^{-1}\cdot\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=0]\leq 2\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=0]\,,

where we used
Lemma

5.6
and the fact
k\cdot\exp(-\Omega((\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out})^{2}/\alpha\sqrt{k}))\leq 1/2
. Note that

\displaystyle\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=a\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=0]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}\in S_{a}}\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}}\ \text{and}\ \mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=0]

(38)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i^{\prime}\in S_{a}}\sum_{j\not\in T_{b}}\Pr_{\mathcal{N}_{i}}[(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i^{\prime}})\geq t_{ij}-\eta]

(39)

We wish to apply
Lemma

5.5
here. Consider any
j\not\in T_{b}
. Note that

\displaystyle t_{ij}-\eta-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-1}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-1}_{j}\rangle-2\eta-E

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{tr}((\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-1}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-1}_{j})-2\eta-E

\displaystyle\geq(1/\beta^{2})\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\|^{2}_{F}-2\eta-E\,.

In
Lemma

5.5
, take
s=(\alpha^{2}/\beta^{3})\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\|_{F}
. Then we can bound the above by

\displaystyle\geq(s\beta/\alpha^{2})\cdot\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\|_{F}\vee\upsilon_{\rm cov}\}+c^{2}(\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out})^{2}/\beta^{2}+c\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}\sqrt{s/\alpha\beta}\,.

By
Lemma

5.5
, this happens with probability at most
O(k)\cdot\exp(-\Omega(s))
. There are at most
k^{2}
terms in the sum in Eq. (
39
), so the claimed bound follows by a union bound.
∎

We can now immediately conclude the proof of the main result of this section:

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

Define
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
as follows. Let
a=\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
b=\mathsf{c}^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))
. If
b=0
, or
S_{a}
and
T_{b}
do not intersect, then define
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
arbitrarily. Otherwise, if they do intersect, let
U_{t}
denote the element of the common refinement of
{\cal S}
and
{\cal T}
corresponding to
S_{a}\cap T_{b}
, and define
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t
.

The bound on the misclassification error then follows from
Lemma

5.6
,
Corollary

5.7
, and
Corollary

5.8
, noting that the condition of Eq. (
17
) ensures that the hypotheses of these components are met.
∎

For convenience, we summarize
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
in
Algorithm

5
below.

Algorithm 5

Clustering

Input:

Partitions
{\cal S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\},{\cal T}=\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\}
of
[k]
, estimates
\{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i})\}
, thresholds
\{t_{ij}\}

Output:

Clustering function
\mathsf{c}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\to[n_{c}]

1

\eta\leftarrow\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}/100\beta^{2}
.

2

Let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{{n_{\rm c}}}
denote the common refinement of the partitions
{\cal S},{\cal T}
.

3

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}
denote the projector to the span of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k}
.

4

Define
c^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
to be the index
a
of the piece
S_{a}
of
{\cal S}
containing
\argmin_{i\in[k]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|
.

5

Define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
to be
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}
for
i=\argmin_{j\in[k]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\Pi}}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|
.

6

Define
c^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
to be the index
b
if there exists
i\in T_{b}
such that
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))<t_{ij}-\eta
for all
j\not\in T_{b}
.

7

if

b=0
or
S_{a}\cap T_{b}=\emptyset

then

8

Define
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
arbitrarily.

9

else

10

Let
U_{t}
denote an element of the common refinement corresponding to
S_{a}\cap T_{b}
.

11

return

\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t
.

12

end if

6
Score simplification

The main difficulty in providing a polynomial approximation of the score function arises when it involves multiple Gaussians that are far apart.
Without further structural assumptions about the function and/or the underlying measure, the degree of the polynomial
approximation depends on (1) the smoothness properties of the target function (e.g., Lipschitz constant or higher-order derivative bounds) and (2) the radius of the support over which the polynomial is guaranteed to be close to the target function.

Recall that the score function of a mixture
\mathcal{M}
of
k
Gaussian distributions with means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}
and covariances

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k}
is given by

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})=-\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 11.38109ptw_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\frac{\lambda_{i}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\,.

For simplicity, in what follows we will denote by
\mathcal{N}_{i}
the
i
-th component
of the above mixture,
\mathcal{N}_{i}=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})
.
For Gaussian mixtures, the effective support of the score function is roughly proportional to the radius of the parameter space which scales with the dimension and the parameter distance
\mathrm{poly}(d,R)
. This is the case as we consider a mixture over
d
-dimensional Gaussians with mean and covariances bounded (in parameter distance) by
R
. Moreover, the Lipschitz constant of the score function can also scale as
\mathrm{poly}(d,R)
. Therefore, applying black-box
polynomial approximation results (such as Jackson’s theorem – see
Lemma

7.2
) would yield a polynomial of degree
at least polynomial in the dimension
d
and the parameter radius
R

yielding a trivial (exponential) runtime. Instead of using the polynomial approximation results in a black-box manner, we will be constructing a piecewise
polynomial approximation of the score function where the partition
is given by the clustering algorithm we designed in
Section

5
.

In this section, we show that given the “rough” clustering function of

Section

5
we can simplify the score function
inside each cell of the partition given by the clustering so that it is possible to prove the existence of a low-degree approximation inside each cell.
More precisely, we require that the clustering function
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
assigns
each
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
to one of
n_{c}
subsets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n_{c}}
of
[k]

that form a partition of the original
k
components such that
if
\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j}
belong in different subsets
U_{t}

and
U_{t^{\prime}}
have to be at least
\mathrm{poly}(\beta/\alpha)\cdot\log(k/\varepsilon)
far in parameter distance. In other words, we require that components in different
subsets of the partition have to be sufficiently separated. Moreover, for every
i\notin U_{t}
, we require that the clustering function
\mathsf{c}
incorrectly classifies a sample
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}
as belonging to
U_{t}
with probability
at most
\varepsilon
. Under those assumptions, we show that for any given

\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t
, we can “simplify” the score function by removing
the contribution of all components
\mathcal{N}_{j}
that do not belong in
U_{t}
.

In what follows, given a subset
U_{t}
of indices of
[k]
we denote by

\mathcal{M}(U_{t})
the submixture containing the components
\mathcal{N}_{i}
for
i\in U_{t}
and by

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
the score function containing only the contribution of
components from
U_{t}
, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))=\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\frac{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\sum_{j\in U_{t}}\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}

The main result of this section is the following proposition showing that,
inside each cell
t
of the partition given by
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
, we can replace the original score function
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})
by the score function of the
sub-mixture
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
.

Proposition 6.1

(Score Simplification)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
\mathcal{M}
be a mixture of
k
Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
with mean and covariances
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}
such that for every pair
i,j

D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}^{2}\leq R

for some
R>1

Moreover, assume that for some
\alpha\leq 1\leq\beta
it holds that

\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
for all
i\in[k]

for
\alpha\leq 1\leq\beta
.

1.

Let
{n_{\rm c}}\in[k]
and let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{{n_{\rm c}}}
be a partition of
[k]
such that for every
i\in U_{t}
, and
j\notin U_{t}
it holds that
D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})
is larger than a sufficiently large absolute constant multiple of
\beta^{4}/\alpha^{2}\log(k\beta/(\alpha\varepsilon))
.

2.

Assume that
\mathsf{c}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto[n_{c}]
is a
\varepsilon
-approximate
clustering function, i.e.,
\pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]\leq\varepsilon
for all
t\in[{n_{\rm c}}]
and
i\notin U_{t}
.

Define the following piecewise approximation to the score function

s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\,.

It holds that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|_{2}^{2}]\lesssim k^{5/4}R\frac{\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Proof.

We first observe that since
\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}=1
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
(i.e.,
each point
x
is only assigned to a single set
U_{t}
),
we can write
s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}
and therefore,
we have that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|_{2}^{2}]=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|_{2}^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}]\,.

We break down the total
L_{2}^{2}
error into the case where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
was actually
generated by a mixture component that belongs to the set
U_{t}
(as
predicted by the clustering function
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
) and the case where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
was
generated by some mixture component that is not in
U_{t}
. Recall that we denote
by
\mathcal{M}^{J}
the joint density of the indexed pair
(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
where
i
corresponds
to the index of the mixture component that generates
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
. We have

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|_{2}^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}]\,

(40)

\displaystyle=\E_{(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t,i\in U_{t}\}]

(41)

\displaystyle+\E_{(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t,i\notin U_{t}\}]\,.

(42)

We first focus on the first part of the error, i.e., when the example
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
is generated by
some component
\mathcal{N}_{i}
that belongs to the set
U_{t}
.
We have

\displaystyle\E_{(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}}

\displaystyle[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t,i\in U_{t}\}]\leq\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}\sqrt{\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}]},

where the last inequality follows by Jensen’s.

We show that as long as a component
\mathcal{N}_{j}
that we remove is far from the
component
i\in U_{t}
in parameter distance, their removal induces an exponentially small
error in the score function.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
be Normal distributions with
means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}
and covariances
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k}

such that for all
i
,
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\leq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
.
For any
i\in U_{t}
, it holds that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|_{2}^{4}]\lesssim\frac{k\beta^{10}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{12}}\sum_{j\notin U_{t}}\exp\left(-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}~D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})\right)\,.

for some universal constant
c>0
. Moreover if
i\notin U_{t}
it holds that

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}

\displaystyle[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|_{2}^{4}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{k}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell}))+\sum_{j\notin U_{t},j\neq i}\frac{k\beta^{10}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{12}}\exp\left(-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}~D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})\right)\,.

Using
Lemma

6.2
we obtain that

\displaystyle\E_{(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}}

\displaystyle[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\mid i\in U_{t}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}}}\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}\sqrt{\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}]}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{k}\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}~\frac{\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}^{3/4}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}}}\max_{j\notin U_{t}}e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}\lesssim\frac{k^{3/4}\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}~\max_{j\notin U_{t}}e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})},

where the last inequality follows from the fact that

\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}^{3/4}\leq{|U_{t}|}^{1/4}(\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i})^{3/4}\leq{k}^{1/4}(\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i})^{3/4}
.
Therefore, using this estimate we obtain that in the case where the sample is generated by some component in
U_{t}
, the error is

\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}\frac{k^{3/4}\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}\max_{j\notin U_{t}}e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}\leq\frac{k^{7/4}\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}\Delta_{\rm out}}\,.

We next bound the error in the difference of the score functions when the clustering function
makes a mistake, i.e.,
\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t
but
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
is generated by
\mathcal{N}_{i}
for
i\not\in U_{t}
.

\displaystyle\E_{(i,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}}

\displaystyle[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t,i\notin U_{t}\}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\notin U_{t}}\lambda_{i}~\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\notin U_{t}}\lambda_{i}~\sqrt{\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}]}\sqrt{\pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\varepsilon}~\sum_{i\notin U_{t}}\lambda_{i}\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha^{4}}\sqrt{\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{k}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})}+\frac{\sqrt{k}\beta^{5}}{\lambda_{i}^{1/4}\alpha^{6}}\sqrt{\sum_{j\notin U_{t},j\neq i}e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}~\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha^{4}}\max_{i\notin U_{t}}\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq i}^{k}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})+\sqrt{D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})})+\frac{k^{5/4}\beta^{5}}{\alpha^{6}}\right),

where for the third step we used the fact that by our assumption it holds
that
\pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]\leq\varepsilon
when
i\notin U_{t}

and for the last inequality we used the fact that there are at most
k
elements that do not belong in
U_{t}
and, similarly to the previous derivation, the fact
that
\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i}^{3/4}\leq{|U_{t}|}^{1/4}(\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i})^{3/4}\leq{k}^{1/4}(\sum_{i\in U_{t}}\lambda_{i})^{3/4}
.
∎

6.1
Proof of Lemma
6.2

We first show the following lemma capturing the effect of removing a single component
from the score function. We show that the induced error is exponentially small in the distance
of the removed component
j
and the component
i
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
be Normal distributions with
means
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{k}
and covariances
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{k}

such that for all
i

\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}

for some
\alpha\leq 1\leq\beta
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be the mixture of
\mathcal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
with weights
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}
.
Let
c>0
be some universal constant. For all
i\neq j
, it holds that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}]\lesssim\frac{k\beta^{10}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{12}}\exp\left(-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}~D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})\right)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}([k]\setminus j))
is the score function
of the mixture after we drop the contribution of component
j
.
Moreover, it holds

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}]\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{\ell}))\,.

By iteratively applying
Lemma

6.3
, and the (almost) triangle
inequality
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}\leq 8\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{a}}\|_{2}^{4}+8\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}

we can remove all the components that do not belong in the set
U_{t}
and obtain the
error guarantee of
Lemma

6.2
.

Proof
Lemma

6.3
.

We first show the following claim bounding the gap between the original score function and the version where we drop the contribution of a component. We remark that the following claim is
a pointwise fact about the score function and holds for every
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
.

Claim 6.4

(Softmax Simplification)
.

Moreover let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{k}
be non-negative weight functions on
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}

and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{k}
be functions
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
.
Define
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/(\sum_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))

and

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{k}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/\Bigl(\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{k}D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\Bigr).

For every
i=1,\ldots,k
, it holds that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|^{4}_{2}\leq 8\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\Bigl(\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}+8\Bigl(\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\,,

where we denote by
A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{k}D_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
.

Proof.

By a direct computation, we observe that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigg(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigg)\,.

Adding and subtracting
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}
, we obtain that the above expression is equal to

\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigg(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\Bigg)\,.

We observe that the normalized weights
D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
form a distribution over
\ell\in[k]\setminus j
and therefore, using Jensen’s inequality, we obtain that

\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\Big\|_{2}^{4}\leq\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\|_{2}^{4}\,.

Combining the above we obtain the following upper bound for the
\ell_{2}
error induced in the score function when we remove the contribution of the
j
-th component. We use the fact
that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}\leq 8\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{a}}\|_{2}^{4}+8\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}
to obtain:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)^{4}\Bigl(8\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\Big\|_{2}^{4}+8\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 8\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}\Bigl(\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}+8\Bigl(\frac{D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\,,

where for the last inequality we used the fact that
D_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/A(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\leq 1
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}

and Jensen’s inequality, since
D_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
is a distribution over
\ell\neq j

and
\|\cdot\|^{4}_{2}
is convex.
∎

Using
Claim

6.4
, with
D
corresponding to
the component
\mathcal{N}_{i}
in the statement of
Lemma

6.3
, we obtain that we have to control the terms

A^{(i,j,\ell)}=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]\,,

(43)

where
S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\sum_{s=1}^{k}\lambda_{s}\mathcal{N}_{s}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
.
Moreover, we have to control the term

B^{(i,j)}=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]\,.

(44)

Using the above notation, and
Claim

6.4
, we obtain that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{2}]\leq 8B^{(i,j)}+8\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}A^{(i,j,\ell)}\,.

(45)

We first bound the term
B^{(i,j)}
. By Cauchy-Schwarz we have

\displaystyle B^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\right)^{2}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\frac{\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}\,,

(46)

where the third inequality follows because the ratio of weighted densities is pointwise smaller than
1
, and the last inequality follows by the fact that
\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/(\lambda_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/(\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
.

We now need to control the following correlation between

\mathcal{N}_{j}
and
\mathcal{N}_{i}
,
\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}\Bigl[\frac{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr]
. We show that as long as the parameters of
\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
are far in
\ell_{2}
from those
of
\mathcal{N}_{j}
this correlation is exponentially small. We prove the following claim.

Claim 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})
be
normal distributions
with
\alpha I\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\leq\beta I
,
\alpha I\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\leq\beta I
.
For
c=16(1+\beta/\alpha)^{2}\beta^{2}
, it holds that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})}\Bigl[\frac{\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})}{\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})+\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})}\Bigr]\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\beta}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{c}~\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\|_{F}^{2}\Bigr)\,.

Proof.

We first observe that we can bound by above the correlation between the two normals
by their Hellinger distance. For brevity, we will denote
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})

as
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})
as
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.
Using the inequality
2tz/(t+z)\leq\sqrt{tz}
we obtain that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}[\mathcal{N}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/(\mathcal{N}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})]\leq\frac{1}{2}(1-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}))
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{H}}^{2}
is the squared
Hellinger distance between
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.
For two normal distributions, we have that

1-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2})=\frac{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}|^{1/4}|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}|^{1/4}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}/2|^{1/2}}\exp(-(1/8)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{u}}^{T}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}/2)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{u}})\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{u}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}
.
Assuming that
\lambda^{1}_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}^{2}
are the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}
,
we observe that we can write

\frac{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}|^{1/4}|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}|^{1/4}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}/2|^{1/2}}=\exp\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{4}\log\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{1}_{i}}{\lambda^{2}_{i}}\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{i}^{1}}{2\lambda_{i}^{2}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\,.

We can now use the following inequality showing that as long as the ratio
\lambda^{1}_{i}/\lambda^{2}_{i}
is not very large the above difference of logarithms
behaves roughly as
(1-\lambda^{1}_{i}/\lambda^{2}_{i})^{2}
.

Fact 6.6
.

Let
x>0
. It holds

\frac{1}{4}\log x-\log(1/2+x/2)\leq-\frac{1}{16}\frac{(1-x)^{2}}{(1+x)^{2}}
.

Proof.

We first use the following integral representation of the logarithm difference

-\frac{1}{4}\log x+\frac{1}{2}\log(1/2+x/2)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{1}^{x}\frac{1}{1+t}-\frac{1}{2t}dt=\frac{1}{4}\int_{1}^{x}\frac{t-1}{(1+t)t}dt\,.

We observe that if
0<x\leq 1
we have that
(1+t)t\leq 2
when
t\in[1,x]
.
In that case, by using the integral identity above, we obtain that
-\frac{1}{4}\log x+\frac{1}{2}\log(1/2+x/2)\leq-(1/16)(1-x)^{2}
.
When
x\geq 1
we similarly obtain the upper bound

-(1/8)(1-x)^{2}/((1+x)x)
. Combining the two cases, we obtain the inequality.
∎

Using
Fact

6.6
we obtain that

\frac{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}|^{1/4}|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}|^{1/4}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}/2|^{1/2}}\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{16C^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\Bigr),

where
C=1+\max_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}^{1}/\lambda_{i}^{2}\leq 1+\beta/\alpha
.
Moreover, since
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}\geq(1/\beta)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
we obtain that

\frac{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}|^{1/4}|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}|^{1/4}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}/2+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}/2|^{1/2}}\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{16C^{2}\beta^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\|_{F}^{2}\Bigr).

∎

In the following claim, we give a bound for the

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]
term that appears in the bound of term
B^{(i,j)}
of

Equation

46
.

Claim 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
,

\mathcal{N}_{2}=\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2})
and define

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2})
.
Assuming that
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\leq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
,
it holds

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}))\,.

Moreover, for
t\geq 2
we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{2t}\Bigr]\lesssim t^{t}\Big(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]\Big)^{t/2}\,.

Proof.

We first observe that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}}\Bigl[\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1/2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2})~\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}})}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}^{T}
with

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1/2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}
.
By
Lemma

4.9
we have that

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}})}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}})^{2}+2\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}\|_{F}^{2}+2\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{2}(1+2\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}))+2\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}\|_{F}^{4}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{2}(1+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}\|_{F}^{2})+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{4}\lesssim(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

We observe that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{S}}\|_{F}=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\|_{F}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\beta}}{\alpha^{2}}~\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\|_{F}
, where the inequality follows by the fact that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}\|_{F}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{B}}\|_{F}
and the spectral bounds on
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}
.
Moreover,
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\|_{2}\leq(1/\alpha)~\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}
, since
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq 1/\alpha
.
Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{1}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{2}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

To obtain the second bound of the claim, we will use the standard hypercontractivity inequality for polynomials (
Fact

6.8
).

Fact 6.8

(Gaussian hypercontractivity)
.

Let
p:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mdmathbb{R}
be a polynomial of degree at most
\ell
and let
t\geq 2
. It holds

\Bigl(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}}[p^{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})]\Bigr)^{1/t}\leq(t-1)^{\ell/2}~\Bigl(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}}[p^{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})]\Bigr)^{1/2}\,.

We have that
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{2}
is a degree
2
polynomial
and therefore the claimed bound follows from the previous bound on

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}=|p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})|^{2}
and the hypercontractivity inequality of
Fact

6.8
.
∎

We can now apply
Claim

6.5
and
Claim

6.7
to the bound of
Equation

46
and obtain the following bound
for some universal constant
c>0
:

\displaystyle B^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{8}}~((\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{2}^{2})~e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}~(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{2}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{8}}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j}))~e^{-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{10}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\alpha^{12}}~e^{-(c/4)\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}\,,

where for the last inequality, we used the fact that for all
t\geq 0
, it holds that

t^{2}e^{-t}\leq e^{-t/4}
and

te^{-t}\leq e^{-t/2}
.

We now bound the cross-error term
A^{(i,j,\ell)}
of Equation
Equation

43
.
We first observe that
A^{(i,j,\ell)}
(in contrast with term
B^{(i,j)}
that we bounded previously) does not vanish when
i=j
. We first focus on the case where
i\neq j
.
Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we obtain

\displaystyle A^{(i,j,\ell)}

\displaystyle=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)^{4}\Bigr]\right)^{1/4}~\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)^{4}\Bigr]\right)^{1/4}~\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\frac{\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr]\right)^{1/4}~\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr]\right)^{1/4}~\left(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{8}\Bigr]\right)^{1/2}\,,

where the third inequality follows because the ratio of weighted densities is pointwise smaller than
1
. We remark that the last inequality holds true because in the case where
i\neq j
it holds that
S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\geq\lambda_{i}\mathcal{N}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})+\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
. We can now use
Claim

6.5

and
Claim

6.7
to bound each of the three terms of the above expression
for
A^{(i,j,\ell)}
separately:

A^{(i,j,\ell)}\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell}))}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{\ell}))\lesssim\frac{\beta^{10}}{\alpha^{12}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}e^{-c^{\prime}\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})}\,,

where
c^{\prime}
is some universal constant and for the last inequality we used the fact that for all
t

where for the last inequality, we used the fact that for all
t\geq 0
, it holds that

t^{2}e^{-t}\leq e^{-t/4}
and

te^{-t}\leq e^{-t/2}
.

Putting together the bounds for
A^{(i,j,\ell)}
and
B^{(i,j)}
we obtain that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}]\lesssim\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}A^{(i,j,\ell)}+B^{(i,j)}\lesssim\frac{k}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}\frac{\beta^{10}}{\alpha^{12}}\exp\left(-c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}~D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{N}_{j})\right)\,.

We now work out the case where
i=j
(see the second estimate in
Lemma

6.3
). Using
Claim

6.4
, for
i=j
, we obtain the following estimate

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}]\leq 8\sum_{\ell=1,\ell\neq j}^{k}A^{(j,j,\ell)}\,.

In this case, we cannot guarantee that the weight
terms
\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})/S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
will be exponentially small and therefore we simply use the fact that they are at most 1:

\displaystyle A^{(j,j,\ell)}

\displaystyle=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{j}\mathcal{N}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{\ell}\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}{S^{-j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})}\Bigr)\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]\leq\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}^{4}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}(D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{\ell})^{2}+D_{p}(\mathcal{N}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{\ell}))\,,

where for the last inequality we used
Claim

6.7
.
Substituting the estimate for
A^{(i,j,\ell)}
yields the claimed bound.
∎

7
Existence and learning of a piecewise polynomial

7.1
Existence of a piecewise polynomial

In this section, we will show the existence of a piecewise polynomial approximation for the score function. To show the desired polynomial existence result, we start by showing the polynomial existence result for the score function of each subset
U_{i}
and combine the results with the clustering guarantee (
Proposition

5.2
) and the score simplification guarantee (
Proposition

6.1
) to obtain the result for the complete mixture.

7.1.1
Polynomial approximation of a sub-mixture with small parameter distance

We will first obtain the result for a mixture
\mathcal{M}(U)
where the mixture has
|U|=m\leq k
components and the parameter distance between any two components
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
for all
i,j\in[m]
. Our main result of this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(U)
be a mixture of
m
well-conditioned Gaussians with
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
and parameters satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
for all
i,j\in[m]
. Let
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be the estimates of the parameters
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\}_{i=1}^{m}
within parameter distance
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\|_{F}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\|_{F}\leq\upsilon
and with the operator norm satisfying
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\alpha}
for all
i\in U
. Then, there exists a polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
of degree
\widetilde{O}(\frac{\beta^{2}m^{2}\upsilon^{5}\Delta_{\rm in}^{6}}{\alpha^{6}\varepsilon})
and coefficients bounded in magnitude by
dR\exp(\widetilde{O}(\frac{\beta^{2}m^{2}\upsilon^{5}\Delta_{\rm in}^{6}}{\alpha^{6}\varepsilon}))
such that for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
, the following holds

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|^{2}]\leq\varepsilon\ ,

where the approximating function is

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\triangleq p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};U)\}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}^{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};U)\}
for some
i\in U
where
\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};U)
denotes the region
\widehat{B}(x;\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})
of the polynomial approximation for cluster
U
.
where
\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}):\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
function that only depends on the estimates
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.

Observe that the score function for the mixture can be written as a product between linear functions (i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})
) and the softmax function. We define the softmax function
w:\mdmathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto[0,1]^{m}
as follows:

w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})=\frac{e^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}_{i}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}e^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{j}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}_{j}}}

(47)

for some fixed parameters
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. We start by showing that in this special case, the score can be pointwise approximated by a low-degree polynomial over a bounded domain (
Lemma

7.4
below).

For this, we will need the following classical polynomial approximation result for functions with bounded gradients:

Lemma 7.2

(Multivariate Jackson’s Approximation,
[
NS64
,
DKN10
]
)
.

For
F:\mdmathbb{R}^{n}\to\mdmathbb{R}
, define the modulus of continuity

\omega(F,\delta)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|_{2},\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\|_{2}\leq 1\\
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\|\leq\delta\end{subarray}}|F(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-F(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})|.

For any
\ell\geq 1
, there exists a polynomial
p_{\ell}
of degree
\ell
such that

\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|_{2}\leq 1}|F(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-p_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})|\lesssim\omega(F,n/\ell)\,.

To prove an upper bound on the coefficients of the polynomial, we will use the following result.

Lemma 7.3

(Coefficients of bounded polynomials,
[
BDBGK18
]
)
.

Let
p
be a polynomial with real coefficients on
d
variables with degree
\ell
such that for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in[0,L]^{d},|p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})|\leq R
. Then, the sum of the magnitude of all coefficients of
p
is at most
R(2L(d+\ell))^{3\ell}
for any
L\geq 1
.

We now show the polynomial approximation result for the softmax function and, as a consequence, for the product of a linear function with the softmax function:

Lemma 7.4

(Polynomial Approximation)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a subset of
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
and
w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})
be the softmax function defined in (
47
). Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})):\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mdmathbb{R}^{d\times m}
be
such that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}\leq M
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
and

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
is linear in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mdmathbb{R}^{m}
with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}=(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))

be such that
|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{r}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})|\leq L
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
.
There exists a polynomial
transformation
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}:\mdmathbb{R}^{m}\mapsto\mdmathbb{R}^{m}
of degree at most

O(LMm^{2}/\varepsilon)

such that for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
it holds that

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})w(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}});\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
.
The sum of the magnitudes of the coefficients of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}
is at most
m\exp(\widetilde{O}(LMm^{2}/\varepsilon))
.

Proof.

The gradient of the softmax function is given by

\frac{\partial w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}_{j}}=\begin{cases}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})(1-w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}))&\text{if }\;\;i=j\\
-w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})w_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We conclude that
\|\nabla w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})\|\leq\sqrt{m}
for all
i\in[m]

and any
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{m}
. Using multivariate Jackson’s theorem (
Lemma

7.2
) for
w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})
, we obtain that there exists a polynomial
q(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})
of degree
\ell
such that

\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\|\leq Lm}|w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})-q(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})|\lesssim\frac{Lm^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\ell}\,.

This implies that we have a set of polynomials
\{q_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})\}_{i=1}^{m}
of degree
O(\frac{Lm^{3/2}}{\varepsilon})

such that for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}
in
L_{2}-
ball of radius
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\|\leq Lm
, we have
\|w(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}};\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}})-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})\|\leq\varepsilon.

Additionally,
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}\leq M
implies that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|\leq M\sqrt{m}
.
Therefore, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}(r(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}))\|_{2}\leq M\sqrt{m}\varepsilon.

We obtain the result by rescaling
\varepsilon
. To obtain the bounds on the sum of the magnitude of coefficients, we use the fact that
|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{q}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})|\leq 2
for all
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}\|\leq Lm
. Therefore, using
Lemma

7.3
, we obtain that the bounds on the sum of the magnitude of coefficients is at most
O((2Lm(m+\frac{LMm^{2}}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{LMm^{2}}{\varepsilon}}))=\exp(\widetilde{O}(LMm^{2}/\varepsilon))
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
be a Gaussian distribution with
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
. Let
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{2},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2})
and
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{3},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})
be any triplets of the same shape as
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})
with condition that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}\|_{\mathsf{op}},\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\alpha}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})\rangle\big|\lesssim\beta\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{F}\log\frac{1}{\delta}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}\|^{2}\big)+\sqrt{\beta}\log\frac{1}{\delta}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|)

Proof.

For
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
, we rewrite
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}^{2}
by writing
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}
for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
, obtaining:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}\|^{2}_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}

(48)

\displaystyle+2(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}}}_{2})-2(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3})

We would like to bound the first two terms in the above equation using Hanson-Wright (
Fact

4.7
). Using
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\|\leq\beta
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\|\leq\beta\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{F}
. Using Hanson-Wright on the quadratic form
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}^{\intercal}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}
, we have for any
\delta>0
that

\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})}\Bigl[|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})\rangle|\gtrsim\beta\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{F}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\Bigr]\leq\delta.

We simplify the sum of the last two terms in (
48
) to obtain

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}}}_{2})

\displaystyle-(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3})=(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2})+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}).

(49)

Using the bounds
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\|_{\rm op}\leq\beta
and
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{\rm op}\lesssim 1/\alpha
, we can upper bound
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2})\|\lesssim\sqrt{\beta}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2})\|\lesssim\sqrt{\beta}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|/\alpha
. So with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}}}_{2})-(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}})^{\intercal}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3})\|\leq\sqrt{\beta}\log\frac{1}{\delta}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|+\frac{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2}\|}{\alpha}).

Putting everything together in (
48
) and assuming
\alpha\leq 1
and
\beta\geq 1
to simplify, we obtain the result.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(U)
be a mixture of
m
Gaussians with well-conditioned covariances
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
for all
i\in[m]
. Let
\Delta_{\rm in}
be an upper bound on the parameter distance between any two components, i.e.,
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}}_{j}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
for all
i,j\in[m]
. Then, for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
and for any
j\in[m]
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\displaystyle|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle|\lesssim\zeta_{1}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{where}\hskip 8.53581pt\zeta_{1}\triangleq\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\;\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|\lesssim\zeta_{2}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{where}\hskip 8.53581pt\zeta_{2}\triangleq\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\;.

Combining it with
Lemma

7.4
, we obtain that there exists a polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
of degree
O(\frac{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}m^{2}}{\varepsilon})

and coefficients bounded in magnitude by
dR\exp(\widetilde{O}(\frac{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}m^{2}}{\varepsilon}))

such that

\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}\big[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|\leq\varepsilon\big]\geq 1-\delta\;.

Proof.

Recall that the score function for the mixture is

s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))=\sum_{i\in U}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636ptw_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\frac{\lambda_{i}\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})^{-1/2}\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}}^{2}}}{\sum_{j\in U}\lambda_{i}\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j})^{-1/2}\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}}}.

We can rewrite the score function as
s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))=s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))+s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})
where
s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
and
s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
are defined as

\displaystyle s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))=\sum_{i\in U}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\text{ and }s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))=-\sum_{i\in U}w_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})

\displaystyle\text{ and }\hskip 8.53581ptw_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}+\log(\frac{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i})}{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})}))+\log\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{1}}}}{1+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}+\log(\frac{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j})}{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})}))+\log\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{1}}}}\,.

We show the polynomial approximation result for
s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
and
s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
using
Lemma

7.4
. To prove an upper bound on
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|
in
Lemma

7.4
, we apply
Lemma

7.5
for all
j,\ell\in[m]
and have that with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell})
(and hence over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
), we have

\displaystyle\big|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\rangle\big|\lesssim\beta\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}\log\frac{m}{\delta}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|^{2})+\sqrt{\beta}\log\frac{m}{\delta}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|).

Using
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\leq 1/\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}=\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{\rm in}/\alpha^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\beta\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\log\frac{m}{\delta}+\frac{1}{\alpha}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|^{2})

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\beta}\log\frac{m}{\delta}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|)\leq\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}

We add and subtract
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle
on the left side and rearranging the terms and

\displaystyle|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}\|_{F}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\,.

We have
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}
. For a fixed
\ell\in[m]
, when
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell})
, we can rewrite
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|
by expressing
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{1/2}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}
for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
to get:

\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|\leq\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|+\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|

(50)

Using Hanson-Wright (
Fact

4.7
), with at least
1-\delta
probability over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
,
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|\lesssim\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|_{F}(1+\log\frac{1}{\delta})\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}
. Using this bound in (
50
), with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, we have

\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|\lesssim\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}.

We apply
Lemma

7.4
to
s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
with the softmax function taking input
r_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=-\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}_{j}=\log\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{1}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})}{\det(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j})}
. We take
L
and
M
therein to be of order
\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
and
\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
respectively. We conclude that there exists a polynomial transformation
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
with degree

O(LMm^{2}/\varepsilon)=O(\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}m^{2}/\varepsilon)

such that with probability at least
1-\delta
over
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, we have

\|s_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|\leq\varepsilon\,.

Note that the multiplication of
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})
to the polynomial approximation of the softmax can increase the sum of absolute values of coefficients at most by a factor of
\frac{dRm}{\alpha}
. The sum of absolute values of coefficients of the polynomial transformation
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
is
\frac{dRm}{\alpha}\exp(\widetilde{O}(\frac{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}m^{2}}{\varepsilon}))
.

We also have
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})\|\leq\Delta_{\rm in}/\alpha
. We apply
Lemma

7.4
for
s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
with the same choice of
r_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
L
but we take
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
and
M
as
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1})
and
\Delta_{\rm in}/\alpha
. Therefore, we obtain that there exists a polynomial
p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
with degree
\frac{\beta m^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}^{3}}{\varepsilon\alpha^{3}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}

such that with at least
1-\delta
probability, we have

\|s_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|\leq\varepsilon\,.

Combining the polynomials
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
and
p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
, we obtain the result.
∎

We define
V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
to measure relative distance of
j^{\text{th}}
input of the softmax to its mean and
