V^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
to measure norm of
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})
as follows:

\displaystyle V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\rangle

\displaystyle V^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})\|^{2}

We similarly define
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}
and
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{2}
using estimates
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
instead of
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\}_{i=1}^{k}
. Define
B(\cdot)
to be the indicator function for whether the input to the softmax is close to its mean and
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})
is sufficiently small in norm:

B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\triangleq\bigwedge^{k}_{j=1}B^{(j)}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636ptB^{(j)}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\triangleq\mathds{1}\Big\{\Bigl(\big|V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\big|\leq\theta_{1}\Bigr)\wedge\Bigl(V^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\leq\theta_{2}\Bigr)\Big\}

Observe that the polynomial approximation result of
Lemma

7.6
holds when
B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=1
. We also define
\widehat{B}
and
\widehat{B}^{(j)}
by replacing
V^{(j)}_{1}
and
V^{(j)}_{2}
with
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}
and
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}
in the definition of
B
and
B^{(j)}
.

Following the parameters used in the proof of
Lemma

7.6
, we will take

\theta_{1}\triangleq\Theta\Bigl(\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\Bigr)\qquad\theta_{2}\triangleq\Theta\Bigl(\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\Bigr)\,.

(51)

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(U)
be a mixture of
m
Gaussians with
\alpha\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}\preceq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}\preceq\beta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}}
and parameters satisfying
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}\|_{F}\leq\Delta_{\rm in}
for all
i,j\in[m]
. Let
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be the estimates of the parameters
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\}_{i=1}^{m}
within parameter distance
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i}\|+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\|_{F}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}\|_{F}\leq\upsilon
and with the operator norm satisfying
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\alpha}
for all
i\in U
.
Then, for any
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the error in estimating
V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
by
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
(similarly
V^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
by
\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\big|V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\omega_{1}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where}\hskip 28.45274pt\omega_{1}\triangleq\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{\alpha}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\;,

\displaystyle\big|V^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{2}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\omega_{2}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where}\hskip 28.45274pt\omega_{2}\triangleq\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}\upsilon^{3}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}\ .

Proof.

The expression of
V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle V^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})-\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=

\displaystyle\big(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle\big)

(52)

\displaystyle-\big(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}\rangle\big)

\displaystyle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle.

Using
Lemma

7.5
by choosing
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1})
as
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell})
and
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{2},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{2},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{2}),(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{3},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{3},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{3})
as
(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1})
and
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{j},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j})
and applying the union bound over
j,\ell\in U
, for
(\ell,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\sim\mathcal{M}^{J}(U)
, with at least
1-\delta
probability, we have

\displaystyle\big|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\beta\upsilon\log\frac{m}{\delta}+\frac{1}{\alpha}(\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}+\upsilon^{2})+\sqrt{\beta}\log\frac{m}{\delta}(\upsilon\Delta_{\rm in}+\frac{\upsilon}{\alpha})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{\alpha}\log\frac{m}{\delta}.

For
j=1
in the above equation, we also have

\big|\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{1}\|_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1}\|_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}\rangle\big|\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{\alpha}\log\frac{m}{\delta}

Note that
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle\lesssim\upsilon(\upsilon+\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}}{\alpha^{2}})
therefore, the last term in (
52
) can be upper bounded as

\big|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}\rangle\big|\lesssim\frac{\upsilon^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}}{\alpha^{2}}\ .

When
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
, using Hanson-Wright (
Fact

4.7
), we have
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|^{2}\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Additionally, we have
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{3}/\alpha^{4}.
Therefore, with at least
1-\delta
probability, we obtain
|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})^{\intercal}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}|\leq\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\Delta_{\rm in}^{3}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
Moreover,
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})\|^{2}\leq\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}/\alpha^{4}
. Therefore, for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})\|^{2}\lesssim\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}.

(53)

Similarly, for any
\ell\in[m],
we have
\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\beta(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}^{2})\lesssim\beta(\upsilon^{2}+\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}/\alpha^{4})
. Using Hanson-Wright inequality (
Fact

4.7
) for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have
\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|^{2}\lesssim(\beta\upsilon^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\log(m/\delta))/\alpha^{4}.
We also have
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j})(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\beta}(\upsilon+\Delta_{\rm in})(\upsilon+\Delta_{\rm in}/\alpha^{2})^{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\beta}\upsilon^{3}\Delta_{\rm in}^{3}/\alpha^{4}.
This implies that with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have
\big|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j})(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})^{\intercal}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\big|\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\beta}\upsilon^{3}\Delta_{\rm in}^{3}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
. We also have
\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j})\|^{2}\lesssim\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}(\upsilon^{2}+\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}})\lesssim\frac{\upsilon^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}
. Combining all the bounds, for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j})\|\lesssim\frac{\beta\upsilon^{3}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}.

Combining this bound with (
53
), we obtain the result.
∎

We now prove our main proposition of this section.

Proof of
Proposition

7.1
.

We set
\widehat{\theta}_{1}=c_{1}\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{2}=c_{2}\frac{\beta\upsilon^{3}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
for some large constant
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=1\}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=0\}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=1\}]

\displaystyle+\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=0\}]

Lemma

7.7
gives us that
|\widehat{V}^{(j)}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})|\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}
implies that
V^{(j)}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\omega_{1}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
and for all
j\in U
and hence,
B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\omega_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2}+\omega_{2})
. We apply
Lemma

7.6
with
\zeta_{1}
as
c_{1}\frac{\beta\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
and
\zeta_{2}
as
c_{2}\frac{\beta\upsilon^{3}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{m}{\delta}
and obtain that there exist a polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))
of degree
O(\frac{\beta^{2}m^{2}\upsilon^{5}\Delta_{\rm in}^{6}}{\alpha^{6}\varepsilon}\log^{2}\frac{m}{\delta})
and coefficients bounded in magnitude by
dR\exp(\widetilde{O}(\frac{\beta^{2}m^{2}\upsilon^{5}\Delta_{\rm in}^{6}}{\alpha^{6}\varepsilon}\log^{2}\frac{m}{\delta}))
such that the following holds:

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=1\}]\lesssim\varepsilon.

We can upper bound the error when
\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=0
using Cauchy-schwarz inequality as follows:

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})\}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigl(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{4}]\Bigr)^{1/2}\big(\Pr[\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=0]\big)^{1/2}

We know that
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2})=0]\leq\delta
. We upper bound the other term as follows:

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{4}]\leq

\displaystyle m^{4}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{4}].

(54)

Writing
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}
in terms of standard Gaussian
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
for any
i,\ell\in[m]
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})-\;\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{4}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}}[\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|^{4}]+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{i})\|^{4}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\|^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{8}}+\beta^{2}\upsilon^{4}+\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}\upsilon^{4}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{8}},

where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

4.9
and
\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|^{4}\lesssim\beta^{2}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{i}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}^{4}+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{1}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}\|_{F}^{4})\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{8}}+\beta^{2}\upsilon^{4}
.
Putting together the above bounds, we obtain that there exists a polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U)}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))\mathds{1}\{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U))=1\}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1})\mathds{1}\{B(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\mathcal{M}(U))=0\}\|^{2}]

(55)

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\varepsilon+\sqrt{\delta}\frac{\beta^{2}\upsilon^{4}\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{8}}

(56)

Choosing
\delta=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\alpha^{16}}{\beta^{4}\widehat{\beta}^{8}\Delta^{8}\Delta_{\rm in}^{8}}
, we obtain the result.
∎

7.1.2
Piecewise polynomial approximation of the complete mixture

The goal of this section is to prove that there exists a piecewise polynomial that can approximate the
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})
. More precisely, there exists
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
when used with the
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}
is
\varepsilon
-approximate to the true score function
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}
, i.e.,

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|^{2}\big]\leq\varepsilon,

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))
is defined as

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}

We will bound the error for every subset
U_{t}
. The error for the subset corresponding to
U_{t}
can be decomposed into an error due to the score simplification of
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{M}(U_{t})
and an error due to the approximation
\mathcal{M}(U_{t})
to the piecewise polynomial score function.

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]

\displaystyle\quad=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]

(57)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big].

(58)

Recall that the score simplification (
Proposition

6.1
) bounds the term in (
57
). We rewrite (
58
) in two parts, when samples are coming from
\mathcal{M}(U_{t})
and
\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})
as follows

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]

\displaystyle\quad=\Pr[j\in U_{t}]\cdot\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t})}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]

(59)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Pr[j\in U_{t}^{c}]\cdot\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]

(60)

The term in (
59
) is upper bounded by
\varepsilon
using
Proposition

7.1
.
In the following Lemma, we upper bound the term in (
60
).

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned mixture and let
U_{t}\subset[k]
be a subset of components. Assume that the clustering function
\mathsf{c}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\to[{n_{\rm c}}]
satisfies
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]\leq\delta
for all
i\notin U_{t}
and
t\in[{n_{\rm c}}].
Then, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]\lesssim\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}k^{3}(\upsilon\Delta_{\rm in}R)^{4}\sqrt{\delta}\;.

Proof.

The term in (
60
) can be upper bounded by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as follows:

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]\\
\leq\Bigl(\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]\Bigr)^{1/2}\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\big]^{1/2}\,.

Using the definition of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
, we can simplify the first term as

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}\big]

(61)

\displaystyle=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{4}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};U_{t})\}\big]

\displaystyle+\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{4}\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}^{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};U_{t})\}]

The first term in (
61
) is upper bounded by
\varepsilon^{4}
. The second term in (
61
) can be upper bounded by

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{4}]\leq k^{3}\sum_{j\in U_{t}}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{4}].

We can upper bound
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell})
by writing it in terms of the standard normal
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})
:

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell})}[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{4}]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Id}})}\big[\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}\|^{4}\big]+\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{j})\|^{4}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}_{\ell}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i})\|^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\|(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{j}^{-1}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{i})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{\ell}^{1/2}\|^{4}+\frac{R^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\widehat{\beta}^{4}(R^{4}+\Delta^{4})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\beta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{\Delta_{\rm in}^{4}}{\alpha^{8}}+\upsilon^{4}\Bigr)+\frac{R^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\frac{(R^{4}+\upsilon^{4})}{\alpha^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}(\upsilon\Delta_{\rm in}R)^{4}.

Additionally, we have

\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}(U_{t}^{c})}\big[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\big]\leq\max_{j\in[k]:j\notin U_{t}}\quad\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{j}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]\leq\delta.

Combining
Equation

61
with the above bound, we obtain the result.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned mixture and then, there exists a piecewise polynomial
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))

such that it satisfies

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|^{2}\big]\leq\varepsilon,

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))
is defined as

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))=\sum_{t=1}^{{n_{\rm c}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))~\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}

and

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))=p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))=1\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{K}}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{j})\mathds{1}\{\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))=0\}\quad\text{for some $j\in U_{t}$ and $\widehat{B}$ defined in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{eq:ball-definition}}

Moreover, every polynomial
p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))
has the degree at most
\mathrm{poly}(\frac{\beta k}{\alpha\lambda_{\rm min}\varepsilon}\log R)
and coefficients of the polynomials are bounded in magnitude by
\mathrm{poly}(d)\exp(\mathrm{poly}(\frac{\beta k}{\alpha\lambda_{\rm min}\varepsilon}\log R))
.

Proof.

Combining
Equation

59
,
Equation

60
and
Lemma

7.8
, for a fixed
t\in[{n_{\rm c}}]
, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathcal{M}(U_{t}))\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\}\big]\lesssim\varepsilon+\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{8}}k^{3}(\upsilon\Delta_{\rm in}R)^{4}\sqrt{\delta}.

(62)

We now combine the bound of the above equation with the score simplification guarantee. The score simplification guarantee (
Proposition

6.1
) assumes that the clustering function
\mathsf{c}:\mdmathbb{R}^{d}\to[{n_{\rm c}}]
satisfies
\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t]\leq\delta
for all
i\notin U_{t}
and
t\in[{n_{\rm c}}].
and obtains that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}[\|s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-s(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|_{2}^{2}]\leq O(k^{5/4}(\beta^{3}/\alpha^{5})R)\,\sqrt{\delta},

Combining the above bound with
Equation

62
, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|^{2}\big]\lesssim k\varepsilon+\frac{\beta^{3}}{\alpha^{8}}k^{3}(\upsilon\Delta_{\rm in}R)^{4}\sqrt{\delta}.

(63)

Using clustering guarantee from
Proposition

5.2
for any
t\in[{n_{\rm c}}],i\in U_{t}
and
t^{\prime}\in[{n_{\rm c}}]
and
t^{\prime}\neq t
, we have

\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t^{\prime}]\leq\Pr_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{i}}[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\neq t]\leq k^{3}\exp\Bigl(-\Omega\Bigl(\frac{(\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out})^{2}}{\alpha\sqrt{k}}\wedge\frac{\alpha^{6}(\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out})^{2}}{\beta^{6}\upsilon_{\rm cov}^{2}}\wedge\frac{\alpha^{2}\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}}{\beta^{3}}\Bigr)\Bigr).

Recall that
\upsilon_{\rm mean}\lesssim\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}
and
\upsilon_{\rm cov}\lesssim k^{3/2}\beta/\lambda_{\rm min}+k^{2}\alpha\log R
. Therefore, we choose
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out}
and
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}
for some large constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
as follows which satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

5.2
.

\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out}=c_{1}\frac{\beta\sqrt{k}}{\lambda_{\rm min}}\log\frac{kR\beta}{\lambda_{\rm min}\alpha\varepsilon}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}=c_{2}\frac{\beta^{4}k^{2}\log R}{\alpha^{3}\lambda_{\rm min}}\log\frac{kR\beta}{\lambda_{\rm min}\alpha\varepsilon}.

We also choose
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm in}\asymp k\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out}
and
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm in}\asymp k\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}
.
Using the chosen values of
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm out}
and
\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm out}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})=t\mid j\notin U_{t}]\leq\varepsilon^{2}\;\mathrm{poly}\Big(\frac{\alpha\lambda_{\rm min}}{\beta kR}\Big)\ .

Using this bound in
Equation

61
, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\sim\mathcal{M}}\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|^{2}\big]\lesssim k\varepsilon.

Rescaling
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon/k
and using
\Delta_{\rm in}=\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}})}_{\rm in}+\Delta^{(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}})}_{\rm in}
and
\upsilon=\upsilon_{\rm mean}+\upsilon_{\rm cov}
in
Proposition

7.1
, we obtain the result.
∎

7.2
Learning polynomials using denoising objective

The goal of this section is to provide details about our learning algorithm using denoising objective. Recall that to sample from the data distribution, the diffusion reverse process uses an approximation to the score function
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
. To learn the score function, we minimize the following DDPM objective in which one wants to predict the noise
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}
from the noisy observation
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
, i.e.

\min_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathcal{G}}\;\;L_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t})=\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}\Bigl[\Big\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\Bigr]\,.

(64)

Given parameter candidates
\{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
and a clustering function
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
, our learning algorithm minimizes the following empirical loss

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}};\mathcal{M}(U_{i}))\\
\forall i\in[k]\end{subarray}\;\;}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}^{(i)})

(65)

\displaystyle\text{where}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}\},

for some large choices of
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}=\mathrm{poly}(dR\tau/\varepsilon)^{\ell}
. Clipping the loss for large values of
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|
is for analysis purposes and in fact, we show that the choice of the value of
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}
and
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}
are sufficiently large such that the unclipped loss will be at most
O(\varepsilon)
in expectation.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned mixture. Then, for any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
and noise scale
t\geq\varepsilon
, there exists an algorithm that runs in
O(d^{\mathrm{poly}(\frac{\beta k\log R}{\alpha\varepsilon\lambda_{\rm min}})}\mathrm{poly}(\log\frac{1}{\delta}))
and returns a score function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}
such that with probability
1-\delta
over samples generated from the mixture
\mathcal{M}
, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\sim\mathcal{M}_{t}}\big[\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|^{2}\big]\leq\varepsilon.

(66)

The algorithm to learn the score function takes input as noise scale
t
, target error
\varepsilon
and confidence
\delta
and it is given by

•

Obtain a candidate list of parameters
\mathcal{W}\leftarrow
CrudeEstimate

•

Brute force over the parameter candidate list
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{1},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{1})\ldots(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{k},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{k})\in\mathcal{W}

–

Brute force over number of mean-based partition (
m
), number of covariance-based partition (
n
), mean-based partition
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{m}\}
and covariance-based partition
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\}

*

Brute force over possible thresholds
\{t_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{k}
in range
[-c\frac{\beta d}{\alpha},c\frac{\beta d}{\alpha}]
for some large constant
c
.

·

Clustering function
\mathsf{c}\leftarrow
Clustering
(S,T,\{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\mu}}}_{i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k},\{t_{i,j}\}_{i,j=1}^{k})

·

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}\leftarrow
minimizer of empirical loss
Equation

65
.

·

Compute the validation loss on the fresh samples for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}
.

In the end, the algorithm returns the
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}
which has minimum validation loss across all brute force candidates.

7.3
Generalization error analysis

As we can decompose the learning problem into learning a polynomial in the piece given by the clustering function
\mathsf{c}(\cdot)
, we can start the generalization error argument by considering the loss function restricted to a fixed piece of the polynomial.

Observe that the DDPM objective can be unbounded in general however, the loss becomes bounded assuming that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}
. Therefore, we first derive the generalization error bound when we restrict the loss function to points
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}
and then argue that the points outside of this region follow with a small probability because of the sub-Gaussian tail of the mixture model outside an appropriate radius.

To simplify the notation, we define the clipped loss and clipped loss restricted to a particular piece as

\displaystyle L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\Big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}\}

\displaystyle L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},U_{i},\widehat{B})

\displaystyle=\Big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})=i,\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},U_{i}),\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}\}.

Similarly define
L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},U_{i},\widehat{B}^{c})
by replacing
\widehat{B}
with
\widehat{B}^{c}
. Recall that for the region where
\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})=i,\widehat{B}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},U_{i})\}=1
,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))
is simplified to
p_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})
.

Lemma 7.11

(Sample complexity)
.

Assume that the sum of absolute values of the coefficient of the polynomial is
M
. Then, choosing
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}=\Theta((\beta Rd/\alpha)\log(1/\delta^{\prime}))
for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
and taking number of samples
n\geq\mathrm{poly}(\frac{dMR\beta}{\alpha\varepsilon t_{\min}}\log\frac{1}{\delta})\mathrm{poly}(\frac{d\beta R}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}})^{\ell}
, with probability at least
1-\delta
over samples, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}^{(i)})+\varepsilon.

Proof.

Denote
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}
as coefficients of the polynomials and
\phi(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})
denote the monomials up to degree
\ell
. Then, we know that
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{\theta}}\|_{1}\leq M
. Additionally, the bound on
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}\|
implies that
\|\phi(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{\infty}\lesssim R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}^{\ell}
. This implies that
\|\phi(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}})\|_{2}\lesssim(dR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}})^{\ell}
. The Lipschitz constant
L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}
for each coordinate can be upper bounded by

\|\nabla L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},U_{i},\widehat{B})\|\lesssim\frac{dMR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}(dR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}})^{\ell}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\leq\frac{dMR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}(dR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}})^{\ell}}{\sqrt{t_{\min}}}.

Additionally, we have
\|L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}\|\leq\frac{(dMR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}})^{2}(dR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}})^{2\ell}}{t_{\min}}
for any
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\leq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}
. We choose
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}},R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}\asymp\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})
for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
and apply standard generalization error analysis result using Rademacher complexity for linear function class (e.g., see
[
SSBD14
]
). If we choose the total number of samples
n
to satisfy
n\geq\frac{(dMR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}})^{4}(dR_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}})^{4\ell}}{t_{\min}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}
,then with at least
1-\delta
probability, we have

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},S_{j},\widehat{B})]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}^{(i)},S_{j},\widehat{B})+\varepsilon

for all
j\in[{n_{\rm c}}]
. Using a similar argument to prove the boundedness of
L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},S_{j},\widehat{B}^{c})
, we also obtain

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},S_{j},\widehat{B}^{c})]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t},S_{j},\widehat{B}^{c})+\varepsilon.

Because
\mathds{1}\{\mathsf{c}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})=j\}
for any single
j
for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}
, combining these bounds for all
j\in[{n_{\rm c}}]
for
n\geq
, we have

\;\;\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L^{({\rm clip})}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}^{(i)})+\varepsilon\ .

∎

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
(\alpha,\beta,R)
-well-conditioned mixture. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
and any noise scale
t\geq t_{\min}\geq\alpha\varepsilon/R
, there exist an algorithm that takes number of samples
n\geq(\log\frac{1}{\delta})d^{\mathrm{poly}(\frac{\beta k\log R}{\alpha\varepsilon\lambda_{\rm min}})}
and runs in sample-polynomial time and returns a score function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}
such that

\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}}[\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}}\log q_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}_{t}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})\|^{2}]\leq\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

We define the loss function outside the radius
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}
or
\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}
as

L^{(out)}_{t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})=\Big\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))+\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}{\sqrt{1-\exp(-2t)}}\Big\|^{2}\mathds{1}\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\vee\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\}

The
L^{(out)}
can be simplified as

\displaystyle\Bigl|\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{(out)}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})]\Bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}}\Bigl[\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathsf{c}(\cdot))\|^{2}\cdot\mathds{1}\Bigl\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\Bigr\}\Bigr]

(67)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{t_{\min}}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}\Bigl[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|^{2}\cdot\mathds{1}\Bigl\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\geq\frac{\beta Rd}{\alpha}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\Bigr\}\Bigr].

(68)

The second term in the above equation can be upper bounded by
(\Pr\{\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|\geq R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}}\})^{1/2}(\E\big[\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}\|^{4}\big])^{1/2}\lesssim\sqrt{\delta}d
. To upper-bound the first term, we first upper-bound
\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}}[\|p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathcal{M}(S_{j}))\|^{4}]
:

\displaystyle\E\big[\|p(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\mathcal{M}(S_{j}))\|^{4}\big]

\displaystyle\leq M^{4}\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}}\big[\|\phi(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t})\|_{1}^{4}\big]\leq M^{4}d^{\ell}\;\Bigl(\max_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\|_{1}\leq 4\ell}\E\big[\prod_{i=1}^{d}|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)}|^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}}\big]\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq M^{4}d^{\ell}\max_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:\|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\|_{1}\leq 4\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\big(\E\big[|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)}|^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}d}\big]\big)^{1/d}

Using Gaussian hypercontractivity (
Fact

6.8
), we can simplify
\E\big[|\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t}^{(i)}|^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}d}\big]\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}d)^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}d}(\beta R)^{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{i}d}\leq(4\ell d\beta R)^{4\ell d}
. Using this bound in (
67
), we have

\Big|\E_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t}}[L^{(out)}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{x}}_{t},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{z}}_{t})]\Big|\lesssim\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}/t_{\min}}d+M^{4}d^{\ell}(4\ell d\beta R)^{4\ell}\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}.

Choosing
\delta^{\prime}=\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon t_{\rm min}/(dM(4\ell d\beta R)^{\ell})
, we obtain the result.
∎
